Grothendieck-Teichmüller Symmetries of Cyclic Operads and Tangles
Marcy Robertson, Chandan Singh

Abstract
. We characterise the profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller group 𝖦𝖳 ^ \widehat{\mathsf{GT}} as the group of automorphisms of the profinite completion of the cyclic operad of parenthesised ribbon braids. This operad admits a metric envelope identified with the category of framed, oriented tangles, thereby providing an operadic model for profinite tangles and their arithmetic symmetries. As applications, we show that 𝖦𝖳 ^ \widehat{\mathsf{GT}} acts naturally on tangles and provide an alternative proof of the formality of the cyclic framed little disks operad.



Let
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1}:=\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1}\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
denote the moduli space of smooth genus–zero curves with
n{+}1
marked points. Since
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, its étale fundamental group

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}\big(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\big)

carries a natural action of the absolute Galois group
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
. Belyi
[
9
]
showed that this action is faithful on
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}\big(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{4}\big)
, and this observation led Grothendieck to propose studying an entire “tower” consisting of all the fundamental groupoids
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n+1})
together with the natural morphisms between them (eg. by forgetting marked points). The hope was that enforcing compatibility with this tower structure would bring one closer to understanding the absolute Galois group itself (
[
32
]
).

In his study of quasi–triangular quasi–Hopf algebras and braided monoidal categories, Drinfeld introduced the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group — in prounipotent,
\mathsf{GT}(\mathbb{K})
, pro-
\ell
,
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}_{\ell}
, and profinite versions
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}

[
20
]
. These groups are characterised by their actions on a “tower” of appropriately completed braid groups. This tower included the collection of all braid groups and the natural maps between them, such as adding or deleting strands. The resulting action of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
on the profinite braid groups mirrors the Galois action on the étale fundamental groupoids of the genus–zero moduli spaces. Ihara
[
44
]
confirmed this connection by exhibiting an inclusion
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}.

The connection between
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
and this tower of braid groups was further solidified in a paper of Lochak and Schneps
[
51
]
who proved that the profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
can be characterised as the group of automorphisms of the tower of profinite braid groups satisfying natural compatibility conditions. Subsequent work of Bar-Natan
[
4
]
, Fresse
[
26
]
, Tamarkin
[
64
]
and others in the prounipotent case, and Horel
[
43
]
, in the profinite setting, clarified that this “tower” structure can be naturally encoded in the structure of an operad of parenthesised braids. Informally, an operad
\mathcal{O}
is a sequence of groupoids (or spaces)
\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(2),\ldots,\mathcal{O}(n),\ldots
equipped with a family of partial composition maps

\circ_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}(m)\times\mathcal{O}(n)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(m+n-1),

describing how to insert one operation into another (Definition
2.1
). For the tower of braid groups, these partial compositions correspond to operations such as adding, deleting, or doubling strands in a braid.

In
[
13
]
, Boavida de Brito, Horel, and the first author showed that, in fact, there is an isomorphism of profinite groups between
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
and the homotopy automorphisms of the (profinite completion of) the operad of parenthesised ribbon braids:

\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}).

The operad of parenthesised ribbon braids
\mathsf{PaRB}
may be viewed as a groupoid model for the framed little discs operad
\mathsf{FD}
in the sense that

\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}(n))\cong\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
for each
n
(see
[
66
, Chapter 1]
). The framed little discs operad is known to admit a cyclic model, reflecting the geometric fact that a configuration of framed discs in the plane need not distinguish the output boundary component.
This raises a natural question: if one imposes compatibility with the cyclic structure—passing from the operad
\mathsf{FD}
to its cyclic enhancement
\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
—does the resulting automorphism group remain the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group, or does the additional cyclic symmetry restrict it to a proper subgroup? Surprisingly, the answer is negative: the extra cyclic symmetry does not reduce the symmetry group.
Indeed, our first main theorem shows that the
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
–action extends to the cyclic operad
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
(Theorem
5.4
; Corollary
5.6
).

Theorem
.

There is an isomorphism between
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
and the homotopy automorphisms of the (profinite completion of) the cyclic operad of parenthesised ribbon braids:

\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}).

The cyclic structure is geometrically significant. Informally, a
cyclic operad
is an operad equipped with an additional symmetry that allows one to permute all
n{+}1
components of an
n
–ary operation, rather than distinguishing a single output (Definition
2.4
). Topologically, the cyclic structure on
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
arises from the geometry of framed configuration spaces.
A configuration of
n
framed discs in the plane determines a point of
\mathsf{FD}(n)
, or equivalently, of the framed configuration space
\mathsf{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Identifying the outer region of the plane with a framed point at infinity places all
n{+}1
boundary components on equal footing; this symmetry is realised by the inversion
z\mapsto 1/z
, which exchanges the inside and outside of the unit disc and allows the ambient disc itself to participate in the operadic composition
[
16
]
.
In this configuration–space model, adjoining the point at infinity compactifies the plane to the sphere
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, mirroring the description of
\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}
as configurations on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
without a distinguished output.

To study how the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acts topologically, we consider its action on the profinite completion of the cyclic operad of framed little discs. The space of
n
framed points in the plane is a
K(\pi,1)
–space whose fundamental group is the pure ribbon braid group
\mathsf{PRB}_{n}
, equivalently the classifying space
\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PRB}_{n}
. Work of Oda (
[
58
]
; Example
4.33
), tells us that there are weak equivalences of profinite spaces

\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PRB}_{n}}\simeq\widehat{\textnormal{B}\Gamma_{0,n+1}}\simeq\Pi^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}),

which relates the profinite completion of the classifying space of the pure ribbon braid group to the étale homotopy type of the moduli space of genus–zero curves with
n{+}1
parameterized boundary components. Thus, the
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
–action on the profinite completion of the cyclic operad of framed little discs induces, through this identification, an action on the étale homotopy types
\Pi^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1})
of the genus–zero moduli spaces.

Applying the classifying-space functor entrywise to the profinite operad of parenthesised ribbon braids then yields a sequence of profinite spaces
\textnormal{B}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)
,
n\geq 1
, but profinite completion of spaces fails to preserve finite products and thus does not commute with the cartesian structures underlying operadic composition. To address this, we pass to a homotopy-coherent setting, replacing strict cyclic operads by cyclic
\infty
-operads whose compositions are defined only up to contractible choice (Section
4
). Following Hackney
[
37
]
, we construct a cyclic
\infty
-operad of framed configuration spaces in Theorem
4.29
,
\widehat{\textnormal{N}\mathsf{S}}_{0}
. This homotopical framework provides a natural setting in which the
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
-action extends from the operad of parenthesised ribbon braids to the profinite homotopy type of framed configurations (Theorem
5.11
)

Theorem
.

There exists an isomorphism of profinite groups

\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}\big(\widehat{\textnormal{N}\mathsf{S}}_{0}\big)\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}\big(\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\big).

The cyclic structure on
\mathsf{PaRB}
has further algebraic consequences. Every operad generates a strict symmetric monoidal category through its enveloping construction, otherwise known as the prop associated to the operad. The presence of a cyclic structure imposes additional symmetries that endow this prop with a form of strict duality as the cyclic rotation that identifies inputs and outputs translates algebraically into the existence of dual objects and evaluation–coevaluation morphisms. In Section
2.3
, we describe the construction of the
metric prop
associated to a cyclic operad which was introduced in
[
41
]
.

The metric prop associated to the profinite cyclic operad
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
yields a category enriched in profinite sets (Example
4.30
) that we identify with a parenthesised version of Furusho’s category of profinite tangles
[
28
]
. The relationship between tangles and
\mathsf{PaRB}
is not completely unexpected. Turaev’s tangle category is the universal strict ribbon (i.e. braided, balanced, and rigid) monoidal category
[
65
]
,
[
49
]
,
[
63
]
and algebras over
\mathsf{PaRB}
are precisely balanced braided monoidal categories
[
66
]
. More recently, the cyclic refinement
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
has been used in the
\mathbb{K}
–linear setting to describe Grothendieck–Verdier categories
[
57
]
. In the profinite setting, we extend the
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
–action—and hence the Galois action—through
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
to Furusho’s profinite tangles (Proposition
6.3
and Corollary
6.10
):

Proposition
.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
acts by
automorphisms on the category of self-dual profinite tangles
q\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
.
In particular, the composite

\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}\longrightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(q\mathbf{T}^{\prime})

defines a nontrivial Galois action on
q\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
.

In Section
7
, we briefly recall the prounipotent version of Grothendieck-Teichmüller group
\mathsf{GT}(\mathbb{K})
. The prounipotent analogue of our Theorem
5.4
and Corollary
5.6
appeared in recent work of Willwacher
[
68
]
, and we use this to show that the constructions of our previous sections recover the Galois actions on tangles anticipated in
[
48
, Appendix D]
(Proposition
7.8
; Corollary
7.9
.

Proposition
.

The
\mathsf{GT}(\mathbb{K})
-action on
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}_{\mathbb{K}}
acts on the category
q\mathbf{T}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{K}}
of parenthesised prounipotent tangles with self-dual objects.

We also conclude with one additional application of the recognition of the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group
\mathsf{GT}(\mathbb{K})
as the automorphisms of a cyclic operad. We use a variation of Petersen’s formality criteria to provide an alternative proof that the cyclic operad of framed discs is rationally formal (Theorem
7.13
).

Theorem
.

The cyclic operad of framed little disks
\mathsf{FD}_{2}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is rationally formal.

Organisation of the paper

Section
1
and Section
2
provide background on monoidal categories, operads, cyclic operads, envelopes, and (metric) props. In Section
3
, we give a full description of the cyclic structure on the operad of parenthesised ribbon braids (Lemma
3.8
). Section
4
describes the essentials of infinity operads and cyclic infinity operads.
In Section
5
, we characterise the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
as the automorphism group of the completed cyclic operad
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
(Theorem
5.4
; Corollary
5.6
). In Section
6
, we construct a nontrivial Galois action on profinite tangles. Section
7
similarly constructs a Galois action on prounipotent tangles (Proposition
7.8
) and proves the formality of cyclic framed little disks operad
\mathsf{FD}_{2}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
(Lemma
7.13
) using
\mathsf{GT}(\mathbb{Q})
–action.

1.
Categorical Background

In this first section, we aim to provide a short, self-contained overview of the categorical and homotopical languages we use throughout the paper. In Section
1.1
, we review the various types of monoidal categories that will be used in the later parts. For a more detailed review, we suggest
[
49
]
or
[
25
]
. In Section
1.2
, we briefly review the background on Quillen model categories and
\infty
-categories, which we will need to discuss maps between monoidal categories and operads up to homotopy.

1.1.
Monoidal Tensor Categories

A
monoidal category

(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1},\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
consists of a category
\mathbf{E}
equipped with a tensor product
\otimes
, a unit object
\mathbb{1}
, and natural isomorphisms: an associator

\alpha_{X,Y,Z}:(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\to X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)

and left and right unitors

\lambda_{X}:\mathbb{1}\otimes X\to X\quad\text{and
}\quad\rho_{X}:X\otimes\mathbb{1}\to X,

subject to coherence conditions expressed via commutative diagrams (the pentagon and triangle identities). A monoidal category is called
strict
if the associativity and unit isomorphisms are identity morphisms.

Throughout, we will assume that our monoidal categories are cocomplete and that the monoidal product
\otimes
preserves small colimits in each variable. A monoidal category is called
simplicial
if it is both tensored and cotensored over the category of simplicial sets. In particular, this means that the ordinary set of morphisms
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)
can be replaced by a mapping space
1
1

1

We follow a common abuse of terminology and use the word
space
to refer to a simplicial set.

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)
for any objects
X,Y
in
\mathbf{E}
. Typical examples of simplicial categories include (completions of) the categories of sets,
\mathbf{Set}
, simplicial sets,
\mathbf{S}
, and groupoids,
\mathbf{G}
. To lighten notation, we will often omit the ambient category
\mathbf{E}
and simply write
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)
or
\operatorname{Map}(X,Y)
when no confusion can arise.

In Section
7
we will work with
\mathbb{K}
-linear categories. A monoidal category
(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
is said to be
\mathbb{K}
-linear
if each hom-set
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)
carries the structure of a
\mathbb{K}
-module and both the composition of morphisms and the monoidal product

(f,g)\longmapsto f\circ g,\qquad(f,g)\longmapsto f\otimes g

are bilinear over
\mathbb{K}
. A functor between
\mathbb{K}
-linear categories is said to be
\mathbb{K}
-linear
if it induces
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps on hom-sets. If we need to emphasize that we are working in the
\mathbb{K}
-linear setting we will write
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(X,Y)
for the vector space of maps from
X
to
Y
.

A
braiding
on a strict monoidal category
(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
is a family of natural isomorphisms

c_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X

satisfying the following conditions for all objects
X,Y,X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z\in\mathbf{E}
and morphisms
f:X\to X^{\prime}
,
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
:

(1.1)

(f\otimes g)c_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}=c_{X,Y}\,(g\otimes f),

(1.2)

c_{X,Y\otimes Z}=(c_{X,Y}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{Y}\otimes c_{X,Z}),

(1.3)

c_{X\otimes Y,Z}=(\operatorname{id}_{X}\otimes c_{Y,Z})\circ(c_{X,Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Y}).

A
strict braided monoidal category
is a strict monoidal category equipped with a braiding. A
strict symmetric monoidal category
is a braided monoidal category with
c_{X,Y}\circ c_{Y,X}=\operatorname{id}_{X\otimes Y}
for all objects
X,Y
.

A
balancing
on a strict braided monoidal category
\mathbf{E}
is a family of natural automorphisms
\Theta_{X}:X\to X
satisfying a braid twist relation

(1.4)

\Theta_{X\otimes Y}=(\Theta_{X}\otimes\Theta_{Y})\circ c_{Y,X}\circ c_{X,Y}.

A
balanced monoidal category
is a braided monoidal category equipped with a balancing.

A
strong monoidal functor
between monoidal categories
(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{E}})
and
(\mathbf{D},\otimes,\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{D}})
is a functor
F:\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{D}
equipped with natural isomorphisms

F(X)\otimes F(Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}F(X\otimes Y)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\cong}F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{E}})

satisfying the standard coherence conditions for associativity and unit compatibility. A functor between strict braided, symmetric, or balanced monoidal categories is a strong monoidal functor that also preserves the additional structure: it intertwines the braidings (respectively, symmetries) and, in the balanced case, satisfies
F(\Theta_{X})=\Theta_{F(X)}
for all objects
X
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
be a strict monoidal category. We say that
\mathbf{E}
has (left)
duality
if, for every object
X\in\mathbf{E}
, there exists an object
X^{*}
together with morphisms

b_{X}:\mathbb{1}\to X\otimes X^{*},\qquad d_{X}:X^{*}\otimes X\to\mathbb{1}

such that

(b_{X}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{X}\otimes d_{X})=\operatorname{id}_{X},\qquad(\operatorname{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes b_{X})\circ(d_{X}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X^{*}})=\operatorname{id}_{X^{*}}.

The pair
(d_{X},b_{X})
implies that the dual
X^{*}
is unique up to a unique isomorphism, if it exists. In a strict monoidal category with left duals, the
transpose
of a morphism
f:X\to Y
is defined as

f^{*}\coloneqq(\operatorname{id}_{Y^{*}}\otimes b_{X})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{Y^{*}}\otimes f\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X^{*}})\circ(d_{Y}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X^{*}}).

Definition 1.2
.

A
strict ribbon category
is a strict balanced monoidal category
(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1},c,\Theta)
equipped with duals, such that the twist satisfies

\Theta_{X^{*}}=(\Theta_{X})^{*}

for all objects
X
.

A
ribbon monoidal functor
,
F:\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{D}
is a strong monoidal functor equipped with a monoidal natural isomorphism

F(X)\otimes F(Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}F(X\otimes Y),\quad\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\cong}F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{E}}),

that is compatible with the braiding, twist, and duality structures of
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{D}
.

Example 1.3
.

The category
\operatorname{Rep}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}))
of finite-dimensional representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
over
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}(q)
, for generic
q
, is a ribbon category. The braiding is induced by the universal
R
-matrix, and the twist (balancing) is given by the ribbon element. Each object has a dual given by the contragredient representation, and the twist satisfies
\Theta_{X^{*}}=(\Theta_{X})^{*}
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
be a strict symmetric monoidal category. An object
X\in\mathbf{E}
admits a
non-degenerate, symmetric form
if there exist morphisms

d:X\otimes X\to\mathbb{1},\qquad b:\mathbb{1}\to X\otimes X

such that the composites

(1.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes\mathbb{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{X}\otimes d}

and

(1.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes\mathbb{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}\otimes X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{X}\otimes b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X}}

are both equal to the identity morphism on
X
. A sufficient condition for such a form to exist is that every
X
is self-dual. See
[
41
, 2.1.1]
or
[
52
]
for further discussion.

Example 1.4
.

If
\mathbf{E}=(\mathsf{Cat},\times,*)
is the category categories of small categories, whose monoidal structure is given by the cartesian product of categories, then the only category
\mathbf{E}\in\mathsf{Cat}
which admits a nondegenerate, symmetric form is the trivial category, i.e.
\mathbf{E}\simeq\ \ast
, see Remark 2.15 of
[
52
]
.

All definitions and constructions above can be weakened by inserting the appropriate associator and unitor isomorphisms into composition formulas. A non-degenerate, symmetric form with weak associativity, say
\alpha:(X\otimes X)\otimes X\longrightarrow X\otimes(X\otimes X)
, gives rise to isomorphism of the
zig-zag
or snake identities (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) to
\operatorname{id}_{X}
. That is

(b\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X})\,\alpha\,(\operatorname{id}_{X}\otimes d)\cong\operatorname{id}_{X}\cong(\operatorname{id}_{X}\otimes b)\,\alpha^{-1}\,(d\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X}).

We note that, by Mac Lane’s Coherence Theorem
[
53
]
, every monoidal category is monoidally equivalent to a strict one. Moreover, the strictification process extends to braided, balanced, and ribbon monoidal categories (
[
47
,
63
]
).

1.2.
Homotopical Preliminaries

In order to discuss homotopy classes of maps between (cyclic) operads, we will make use of the language of Quillen model categories and of
\infty
-categories. For model categories, we take
[
42
]
or
[
3
]
as comprehensive references. To model
\infty
-categories, we use the formalism of
relative categories
from
[
6
]
or
[
3
, Chapter 13]
.

Definition 1.5
.

A
relative category
is a pair
(\mathbf{E},\mathscr{W})
, where
\mathbf{E}
is a small category and
\mathscr{W}
is a wide subcategory of
\mathbf{E}
whose morphisms are a distinguished class of
weak equivalences
. A functor of relative categories

F\colon(\mathbf{E},\mathscr{W})\longrightarrow(\mathbf{E}^{\prime},\mathscr{W}^{\prime})

is a functor
F\colon\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
such that
F(\mathscr{W})\subseteq\mathscr{W}^{\prime}
.

Any model category
\mathbf{E}
determines a relative category
(\mathbf{E},\mathscr{W})
, where
\mathscr{W}
is the class of weak equivalences in the model structure on
\mathbf{E}
. The
homotopy category
of a relative category
\mathbf{E}
, denoted
\operatorname{Ho}(\mathbf{E})
, is obtained by formally inverting the morphisms in
\mathscr{W}
.

The Dwyer–Kan simplicial localisation of
\mathbf{E}
, denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E},\mathscr{W})
, is a simplicially enriched category that has the same objects as
\mathbf{E}
and, for any
X,Y\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{E})
, a space of maps
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(X,Y)
. Here,
\mathbb{R}
indicates the right-derived version of the functor
\operatorname{Map}(-,-)
2
2

2

This is also often denoted
\operatorname{Map}^{h}(X,Y)
in the literature, e.g.
[
42
]
. By viewing
\mathbf{E}
as a discrete simplicial category, one obtains a natural inclusion

\mathbf{E}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E},\mathscr{W})

with the property that, for all
X,Y\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{E})
,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ho}(\mathbf{E})}(X,Y)\,=\,\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y).

In other words, the connected components of
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)
correspond to morphisms in the homotopy category
\operatorname{Ho}(\mathbf{E})
and the higher simplices of
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)
capture higher homotopical information. In particular, for any weak equivalence
X\to X^{\prime}
and any object
Y
in
\mathbf{E}
, the induced maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\prime},Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(Y,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(Y,X^{\prime})}

are weak equivalences of spaces (cf.
[
24
]
).

Although derived mapping spaces
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)
are often difficult to compute, when
\mathbf{E}
is a simplicial model category (cf.
[
42
, Chapter 9]
), we have the following useful characterisation:

Theorem 1.6

(
[
23
, Corollary 4.7]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a simplicial model category. If
X
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant in
\mathbf{E}
, then there exists a natural zigzag of weak equivalences

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y)\,\simeq\,\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,Y).

Given two simplicial model categories
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{D}
, a simplicial Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

induces a homotopy adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E},\mathscr{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{D},\mathscr{W}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}G}

in the homotopy categories of relative categories. Concretely, for all
X\in\mathbf{E}
and
Y\in\mathbf{D}
there is a natural weak equivalence of mapping spaces

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathbb{L}F(X),Y)\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{E}}(X,\mathbb{R}G(Y)),

where
\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{R}
denote the left and right derived functors of
F
and
G
, respectively.

As a particular example of such an adjunction, let
\mathbf{S}
denote the category of simplicial sets with its standard Kan–Quillen simplicial model structure (cf.
[
3
, Chapter 6.1]
), and let
\mathbf{G}
denote the category of groupoids equipped with the model structure in which weak equivalences are equivalences of categories, cofibrations are morphisms injective on objects, and fibrations are isofibrations (see
[
1
]
,
[
14
]
).

The functor sending a simplicial set
X
to its fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(X)
is left adjoint to the
classifying space
functor:

(1.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}\colon\mathbf{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}\colon\textnormal{B}.}

This adjunction endows
\mathbf{G}
with a simplicial model structure by transport of structure, with

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}):=\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{S}}(\textnormal{B}\mathbf{E},\textnormal{B}\mathbf{D})

for all groupoids
\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}
. The classifying space functor preserves and reflects weak equivalences and fibrations, and the adjunction (
1.7
) is a simplicial Quillen adjunction whose right adjoint is
homotopically fully faithful
, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathbf{S}}(\textnormal{B}\mathbf{E},\textnormal{B}\mathbf{D})}

is a weak equivalence for all
\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}\in\mathbf{G}
.

2.
Operads and cyclic operads

We review operads and cyclic operads in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
, and explain how an operad generates a strict symmetric monoidal category through its envelope in Section
2.3
. We then build on this framework to introduce the metric prop of a cyclic operad, which augments the envelope by formally adjoining duals in Section
2.4
.

Throughout the section we assume
\mathbf{E}=(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
be a closed symmetric monoidal category in which the tensor product
\otimes
commutes with all colimits and let
\Sigma_{n}=\operatorname{Aut}\{1,\ldots,n\}
denote the symmetric group on
n
letters.

2.1.
Operads

A
symmetric sequence
in
\mathbf{E}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-graded collection of objects

\mathcal{O}=(\mathcal{O}(0),\mathcal{O}(1),\ldots,\mathcal{O}(n),\ldots),

where each
\mathcal{O}(n)
carries a right action
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\times\Sigma_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}}

Definition 2.1
.

An
operad
in
\mathbf{E}
consists of a symmetric sequence
\mathcal{O}=\{\mathcal{O}(n)\}_{n\geq 0}
in
\mathbf{E}
together with:

(1)

a distinguished element
1\in\mathcal{O}(1)
called the
unit
;

(2)

a family of associative, equivariant and unital partial compositions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\times\mathcal{O}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n+m-1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}

where
1\leq i\leq n
.

The composition operation
\circ_{i}
represents the substitution of an
m
-ary operation into the
i^{th}
input of an
n
-ary operation. This composition is often depicted by grafting rooted trees as in Figure
1
.
Associativity and equivariance ensure the coherence of multiple such substitutions, while the unit provides an identity for composition.

A morphism
f:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
of operads is a map of the underlying symmetric sequences that commutes with the additional operad structure. The category of operads in
\mathbf{E}
is denoted by
\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})
. For further details see
[
26
, Chapter 1]
or
[
54
]
.

1
2
4
5
3
1
4
2
3
1
2
3
8
4
5
6
7
\circ_{4}
=

Figure 1.
The partial composition
\circ_{4}:\mathcal{O}(5)\times\mathcal{O}(4)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(8)

Example 2.2
.

Fix an object
X\in\mathbf{E}
and define
\operatorname{End}(X)(n):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\otimes n},X)
. This gives a symmetric sequence
\operatorname{End}(X)=\{\operatorname{End}(X)(n)\}_{n\geq 0}
, with a natural
\Sigma_{n}
-action

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{End}(X)(n)\times\Sigma_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{End}(X)(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}}

given by permuting the inputs of a function
f:X^{\otimes n}\rightarrow X
,

\sigma^{*}(f):=f(x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(n)}).

The composition maps are defined via function composition:

\left(f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),g(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})\right)\overset{\circ_{i}}{\longmapsto}f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},g(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}),x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n}).

The identity morphism
\operatorname{id}_{X}
serves as the operadic unit.

An
algebra
over an operad
\mathcal{O}
is an operad morphism
\rho:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(A)
. Note that this is equivalent to giving an action

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes A^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}

for each
n\geq 1
. A morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras is a map
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathbf{E}
which is compatible with the
\mathcal{O}
-action. We write
\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbf{E})
for the category of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras in
\mathbf{E}
.

Example 2.3
.

The
little
2
-disks operad

\mathsf{D}_{2}
is an operad in the category of topological spaces whose
n
th space
\mathsf{D}_{2}(n)
consists of all smooth, orientation-preserving embeddings

\coprod_{k=1}^{n}D^{2}_{k}\,\longrightarrow\,D^{2}

of
n
disjoint disks into the unit disk, where each embedding is a composition of a translation and a dilation.
Operadic composition is given by inserting one configuration of disks into another, corresponding to the composition of embeddings.
Locally,
\mathsf{D}_{2}(n)
models the configuration space of
n
distinct points in the plane,

\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C}):=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{\times n}\mid z_{i}\neq z_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j\},

in the sense that there is a weak homotopy equivalence
\mathsf{D}_{2}(n)\simeq\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
for all
n\geq 1
.

The
framed little
2
-disks operad
,
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
, extends
\mathsf{D}_{2}
by allowing each disk to rotate.
Equivalently,
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
is the semidirect product of operads

\mathsf{FD}_{2}\simeq\mathsf{D}_{2}\rtimes\mathsf{SO}(2)

(
[
66
]
,
[
62
]
).
The operad
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
provides a model for an operad of configurations of framed points in the plane,

\mathrm{Conf}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathbb{C}):=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})\mid(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C}),\,\theta_{i}\in S^{1}\},

with each
\theta_{i}
recording a framing (or tangent direction) at
z_{i}
. That is to say, for every
n\geq 1
there is a weak homotopy equivalence

\mathsf{FD}_{2}(n)\simeq\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

2.2.
Cyclic operads

Cyclic operads remove the distinction between inputs and outputs of operations. Equivalently, a cyclic structure on an operad encodes the data required to describe invariant bilinear forms on its algebras (
[
29
]
).

We write
\Sigma_{n}^{+}=\operatorname{Aut}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})
for the
extended symmetric group
. We note that
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
contains
\Sigma_{n}
as the subgroup of automorphisms that fix zero. Let
z_{n+1}
denote the cyclic permutation
z_{n+1}(i)=i+1\ (\text{mod}\ n+1)
that generates a copy of the cyclic group in
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
.

A
cyclic structure
on an operad
\mathcal{O}
consists of action maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\times\Sigma_{n}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}}

n\geq 1
, which satisfy the following properties.

(1)

If
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}\subset\Sigma^{+}_{n}
, then the action
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\times\Sigma_{n}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}}
is the action coming from the operad structure on
\mathcal{O}
.

(2)

For every
x\in\mathcal{O}(n)
and
y\in\mathcal{O}(m)
, we have the following:

(2.1)

~z_{n+m}^{*}(x\circ_{i}y)=\begin{cases}z_{n+1}^{*}(x)\ \circ_{i-1}\ y\ \ \text{if}\ \ 2\leq i\leq n\\
z_{m+1}^{*}(y)\ \circ_{m}\ z_{n+1}^{*}(x)\ \ \text{if}\ \ i=1\ \text{and}\ n\not=0.\end{cases}

The identity (
2.1
) ensures that rotating an operation and then composing is the same as composing and then rotating, guaranteeing compatibility between cyclic symmetry and operadic substitution. Note that in our definition of a cyclic structure, we have excluded the case
i=1
,
m=0
from the definition, because you can deduce the formula
z_{n+m}^{*}(x\circ_{1}y)=((z_{n+1}^{2})^{*}x)\circ_{n}y
from the first case.

2
3
4
5
1
0
3
4
2
5
0
1
\overset{z^{*}}{\longrightarrow}

Figure 2.
A cyclic action
z^{*}
that permutes the first input into the output and the output with the last input.

Definition 2.4
.

A
cyclic operad
is an operad
\mathcal{O}=\{\mathcal{O}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
equipped with a cyclic structure.

A map
f:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
of cyclic operads is a morphism of the underlying operads that commutes with the additional cyclic structure. We denote the category of cyclic operads in
\mathbf{E}
by
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})
.

Example 2.5
.

Let
X\in(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
be an object equipped with a non-degenerate, symmetric, bilinear form
d:X\otimes X\to\mathbb{1}
. The endomorphism operad
\operatorname{End}(X)
(Example
2.2
) has

\operatorname{End}(X)(n):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\otimes n},X).

The bilinear form
d
allows us to identify
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\otimes n},X)
with
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\otimes n+1},\mathbb{1})
, by setting

f\mapsto(f\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ d:X^{\otimes n}\otimes X\to\mathbb{1}.

This identification equips
\operatorname{End}(X)
with a
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
-action, where the generator
z_{n+1}
of the cyclic group acts by cyclically permuting the inputs and the “output” position. The resulting operad is called the
cyclic endomorphism operad

\operatorname{End}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(X)
.

An
algebra
over a cyclic operad
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is a morphism of cyclic operads
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\operatorname{End}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(X)
. Equivalently, an algebra over
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
in
\mathbf{E}:=(\mathbf{E},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
consists of
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
X
together with a non-degenerate symmetric form
d:X\otimes X\rightarrow\mathbb{1}
which is
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
-
invariant
in the sense that the composites

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)\otimes X^{\otimes n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

are
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
-equivariant for all
n\geq 1
.

Example 2.6
.

In
[
57
, Section 5.2]
the authors describe a topological operad of genus zero surfaces based on Budney’s operad of conformal balls
[
15
]
.

The topological cyclic operad
\mathsf{S}_{0}
has
n
–ary operations given by tuples of
admissible embeddings

\mathsf{S}_{0}(n)=\bigl\{(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\,\bigm|\,a_{i}\colon D^{2}\hookrightarrow S^{2}\bigr\},

where each
a_{i}
satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

a_{i}
is an orientation–preserving smooth embedding of the open unit disc
D^{2}
into
S^{2}
;

(ii)

the image
a_{i}(D^{2})
does not contain the antipodal point of its centre
a_{i}(0)
, so that the stereographic projection from this antipodal point is well defined on
a_{i}(D^{2})
;

(iii)

the closures
\overline{a_{i}(D^{2})}
have pairwise disjoint interiors.

The topology on
\mathsf{S}_{0}(n)
is induced from the compact open topology on the space of embeddings, and the extended symmetric group
\Sigma_{n+1}
acts by permuting the labels of the discs.

For
a=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{S}_{0}(n)
and
b=(b_{0},\ldots,b_{m})\in\mathsf{S}_{0}(m)
, the composition
a\circ_{i}b\in\mathsf{S}_{0}(n+m)
is defined by inserting the configuration
b
into the
i
th disc of
a
. Concretely, one applies stereographic projection from the antipodal point of
a_{i}(0)
to identify
a_{i}(D^{2})
with the complex plane, rescales the configuration
b
to fit inside that image, and then embeds it back into
S^{2}
via
a_{i}
. This composition is continuous, associative up to the evident relabelling of discs, and equivariant for the
\Sigma_{n+1}
–action, endowing
\mathsf{S}_{0}
with the structure of a topological cyclic operad.

There’s a natural map
\mathsf{FD}_{2}(n)\rightarrow\mathsf{S}_{0}(n)
which embeds a configuration of discs in
\mathsf{FD}_{2}(n)
into the bottom hemisphere of
S^{2}
via the inverse of stereographic projection along the north pole of
S^{2}
and then adds the upper hemisphere of
S^{2}
as the
0
th disc. This defines a weak homotopy equivalence of (non-cyclic) operads

\mathsf{FD}_{2}\simeq\mathsf{S}_{0}.

The
\Sigma_{n+1}
–symmetry on
\mathsf{S}_{0}
then pulls back along this equivalence to induce a cyclic structure on the framed little disks operad, identifying the outer and inner boundaries of a framed configuration on
S^{2}
. We will write
\mathsf{FD}_{2}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
if we want to emphasize that
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
is carrying this cyclic structure.

Example 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}
denote the moduli space of smooth genus-zero curves with
(n{+}1)
distinct marked points, and let
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n+1}
be its Deligne–Mumford compactification.
The collection

\bar{\mathcal{M}}:=\{\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n+1}\}_{n\geq 2}

forms a
cyclic operad
in spaces (
[
29
]
).
A point of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n+1}
is a tuple
(C;x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})
where

C\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
x_{i}\in C
are distinct marked points, considered up to
biholomorphic automorphisms (i.e. Möbius transformations) of
C
.
The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n+1}
acts by permuting the labels of the marked points.

The composition operation

(C;x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\circ_{i}(D;y_{0},\ldots,y_{m})=\big(C\cup D/(x_{i}\sim y_{0});x_{0},\ldots,x_{i-1},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n}\big)

glues the
i
th marked point of
C
to the distinguished point
y_{0}
of
D
to form a nodal genus-zero curve, producing an element of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n+m}
.

2.3.
The envelope of an operad

Every operad generates a strict symmetric monoidal category called a prop. A
prop

\mathcal{P}
is a strict symmetric monoidal category whose objects are the natural numbers
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
.The morphism set
\mathcal{P}(m,n)
is interpreted as the space of operations with
m
inputs and
n
outputs.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an operad in
\mathbf{E}
. The
envelope

\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})
is a strict symmetric monoidal category whose objects are natural numbers and whose morphisms are defined by:

(2.3)

\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})(m,n):=\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}=m\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{O}(m_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}(m_{n})\right)\underset{\Sigma_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{m_{n}}}{\otimes}\Sigma_{m},

where the coproduct runs over all partitions of
m
into
n
parts. The Eq (
2.3
) is a coproduct of coequalisers where the
\Sigma_{m_{1}}\times\ldots\times\Sigma_{m_{n}}
-action on
\Sigma_{m}
is given by left multiplication on the image of
\Sigma_{m_{1}}\times\ldots\times\Sigma_{m_{n}}
in
\Sigma_{m}
and right
\Sigma_{m_{i}}
-action on
\mathcal{O}(m_{i})
.
3
3

3

The notation

X\underset{H}{\otimes}Y:=\mathrm{coeq}\left(\coprod_{h\in H}X\otimes Y\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\xrightarrow{h\otimes\mathrm{id}}\\[-1.99997pt]
\xrightarrow[\mathrm{id}\otimes h]{}\end{subarray}}X\otimes Y\right)

denotes the coequalizer that enforces
H
-equivariance on the tensor product.

A morphism in
\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})(m,n)
can be represented by a pair

((f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}),\sigma),

where each
f_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(m_{i})
, the
m_{i}
sum to
m
, and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
acts by permuting the inputs. For any two morphisms
g\in\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})(m;n)
and
f\in\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})(p;q)
, their monoidal product
g\ \boxtimes\ h\in\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})(m+p;n+q)
is defined by concatenation:

(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{1},\ldots,g_{q};\sigma\oplus\sigma^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})(m+p,n+q),

where
\sigma\oplus\sigma^{\prime}
is the image under the canonical embedding
\Sigma_{m}\times\Sigma_{p}\hookrightarrow\Sigma_{m+p}
. See, for example,
[
60
]
for more details on this construction.

This construction defines the left adjoint in an adjunction between operads and props:

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{Prop}(\mathbf{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Env}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Here, the right adjoint
u
sends a prop to its underlying operad by extracting the operations with one output:
u(\mathcal{P})(n):=\mathcal{P}(n,1)
. See, for example,
[
41
, 2.6.1]
or
[
36
, Proposition 11]
for more details.

An
algebra over a prop

\mathcal{P}
is a symmetric monoidal functor
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbf{E}
such that
F(1)=X
. The object
X
is then said to carry a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra structure. The category of such algebras is denoted
\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathcal{P})
. If the prop in question is generated by an operad, the notion of algebras coincide in the following sense.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an operad in
\mathbf{E}
, and let
\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})
be its envelope. Then there is a natural isomorphism of categories:

\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})).

2.4.
The envelope of a cyclic operad

To encode invariant bilinear forms at the level of props, one can extend the envelope construction using data from a cyclic operad. The resulting object is called a
metric prop
in
[
41
, 3.1.3]
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
be a cyclic operad whose underlying operad is
\mathcal{O}
. The
metric prop
associated to
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
, denoted
\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
, is the prop associated to the underlying operad
\operatorname{Env}(\mathcal{O})
together with two additional generating morphisms

d\in\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})(2,0)\quad\text{and}\quad b\in\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})(0,2)

for which the composites

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\cong 0\otimes 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 1\otimes 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 0\cong 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{1}\otimes d}

and

(2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\cong 1\otimes 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 1\otimes 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\otimes 1\cong 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{1}\otimes b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1}}

compose to the identity map in
\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})(1,1)
. Moreover, we require that for any operation
f\in\mathcal{O}(n)
, the composite

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n\cong 0\otimes n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 1\otimes n=1\otimes n\otimes 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 1\otimes 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes 0\cong 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes\operatorname{id}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{1}\otimes f\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{1}\otimes d}

agrees with the cyclic action
z^{*}(f)
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)
.

An
algebra over the metric prop

\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
is a symmetric monoidal functor
F:\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\to\mathbf{E}
such that
F(1)=X
and the images

F(d):X\otimes X\to\mathbb{1}\quad\text{and}\quad F(b):\mathbb{1}\to X\otimes X

define a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form on
A
. The category of such algebras is denoted
\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}))
. The algebras over a cyclic operad
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
and the associated metric prop
\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
coincide.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
41
]
, Lemma 3.1.4)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
be a cyclic operad in
\mathbf{E}
. Then there is an equivalence of categories:

\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\Pi(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}))\cong\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{cyc}}).

3.
A cyclic operad of ribbon braids

Campos, Idrissi, and Willwacher observed in
[
16
]
that the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids
\mathsf{PaRB}
carries a cyclic structure induced by the involution on the configuration spaces of framed points in
\mathbb{C}
given by
z\mapsto 1/z
. This action extends the cyclic structure introduced by Müller and Woike
[
57
]
on a groupoid model for the cyclic structure on
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
(Example
2.6
). We recall some background on braids and ribbon braids in Section
3.1
and Section
3.2
and give a full description of the cyclic structure on the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids in Section
3.3
.

3.1.
Braids and ribbon braids

The configuration space of
n
unordered points in the complex plane,
\mathsf{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})/\Sigma_{n},

has fundamental group equal to the
braid group
on
n
strands:
\mathsf{B}_{n}:=\pi_{1}(\mathsf{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})/\Sigma_{n},p).

A standard presentation of
\mathsf{B}_{n}
is

\mathsf{B}_{n}=\left\langle\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\beta_{i}\beta_{j}=\beta_{j}\beta_{i}\quad\text{for }|i-j|\geq 2,\\[2.0pt]
\beta_{i}\beta_{i+1}\beta_{i}=\beta_{i+1}\beta_{i}\beta_{i+1}\end{array}\right\rangle.

The generators
\beta_{i}
represent elementary exchanges of adjacent points in the configuration.

For each
n\geq 1
there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PB}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\pi
records the induced permutation of strands.
The kernel
\mathsf{PB}_{n}
is the
pure braid group
, which identifies with the fundamental group of the ordered configuration space:
\mathsf{PB}_{n}=\pi_{1}(\mathsf{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C}),p).

It is generated by the full twists

x_{ij}=\beta_{j-1}\cdots\beta_{i+1}\beta_{i}^{2}\beta_{i+1}^{-1}\cdots\beta_{j-1}^{-1},

1\leq i<j\leq n
.

Similarly, the configuration space of unordered
framed
points in the plane,
\mathsf{Conf}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathbb{C})/\Sigma_{n},

has fundamental group given by the
ribbon braid group
:

\mathsf{RB}_{n}:=\pi_{1}(\mathsf{Conf}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathbb{C})/\Sigma_{n},p).

This group is generated by the braid generators

\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}
together with
twists

\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}
, satisfying the braid relations and the twist relations

\beta_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\beta_{i}\quad(j\notin\{i,i+1\}),\qquad\beta_{i}\tau_{i+1}=\tau_{i}\beta_{i},\qquad\tau_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\tau_{i}\quad(i\neq j).

The kernel of the projection
\mathsf{RB}_{n}\to\Sigma_{n}
is the
pure ribbon braid group
,

\mathsf{PRB}_{n}:=\pi_{1}(\mathsf{Conf}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathbb{C}),p),

the fundamental group of the ordered framed configuration space.

3.2.
The operad of parenthesized ribbon braids

The operad of
coloured ribbon braids
,
\mathsf{CoRB}
, is obtained by assembling the pure ribbon braid groups
\mathsf{PRB}_{n}
into an operad. It serves as a groupoid model for the operad of framed little disks
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
in the sense that, for each
n\geq 1
, there is a natural equivalence of groupoids

\mathsf{CoRB}(n)\,\simeq\,\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}_{2}(n)).

The
parenthesized ribbon braid
operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
is a cofibrant replacement of
\mathsf{CoRB}
, providing a homotopically well-behaved model for the operad built from the fundamental groupoids
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})=\{\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}_{2}(n))\}
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
n\geq 1
, we define a symmetric sequence of groupoids
\mathsf{CoRB}(n)
with:

•

\operatorname{ob}(\mathsf{CoRB}(n))=\Sigma_{n}
;

•

morphisms
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{CoRB}(n)}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
are elements of the ribbon braid group
\mathsf{RB}_{n}
whose underlying permutation is
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{-1}
.

Categorical composition is given by the multiplication of ribbon braids. Using the group isomorphism,
\mathsf{RB}_{n}\cong\mathsf{B}_{n}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}^{\times n}
, we can write any morphism
r\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{CoRB}(n)}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
as a pair
r=(\gamma,[\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}])
where
\gamma
is a braid on
n
-strands with underlying permutation
\pi(\gamma)=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{-1}\in\Sigma_{n}
. The tuple
[\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{\times n}
is the data of the “twists”, where
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
is the number of twists of the
i
th ribbon strand.

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts naturally on the objects of each groupoid
\mathsf{CoRB}(n)
and the collection
\mathsf{CoRB}=\{\mathsf{CoRB}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
forms a symmetric operad in the category of groupoids. The operadic composition functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CoRB}(n)\times\mathsf{CoRB}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CoRB}(n+m-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}

are defined at the level of objects as composition of permutations. Informally,
\sigma\circ_{k}\tau
consists of placing
\tau\in\Sigma_{m}
into place
k
of
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
, e.g.
(123)\circ_{2}(21)=(1{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}32}4)
. See
[
13
, Definition 6.1]
or
[
26
, Section 5.2]
.

Operadic composition of morphisms is given by inserting a ribbon braid from
\mathsf{RB}_{m}
into the
k
th strand of a ribbon braid in
\mathsf{RB}_{n}
. To be more explicit, we first introduce some notion: for an integer
m\geq 0
, we write
\mathbf{R}_{m}
for the
m
th full twist
(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{m-1})^{m}
in
\mathsf{PB}_{m}
. Given morphisms
r_{1}=(\gamma_{1},[\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}])\in\mathsf{CoRB}(n)
and
r_{2}=(\gamma_{2},[\tau^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tau^{\prime}_{m}])\in\mathsf{CoRB}(m)
then

r_{1}\circ_{1}r_{2}:=(\omega,[\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{i-1},\tau_{i}+\tau^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tau_{i}+\tau^{\prime}_{m},\tau_{i+1},\ldots\tau_{n}])\in\mathsf{CoRB}(n+m-1)

where
\omega:=\gamma_{1}\circ_{i}((\mathbf{R}_{m})^{\tau_{i}}\cdot\gamma_{2})
and
(\mathbf{R}_{m})^{x_{k}}
is the
x_{k}
-fold categorical composition of
\mathbf{R}_{m}
considered as an element in the braid group
\mathsf{B}_{n}
. See, for example,
[
13
, Definition 6.4]
for more details.

The operad of coloured ribbon braids
\mathsf{CoRB}
provides a groupoid model for the operad of framed discs
\mathsf{FD}
, in the sense that applying the fundamental groupoid functor entrywise yields an isomorphism

\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}(n))\cong\mathsf{CoRB}(n)

for each
n
(
[
66
, Chapter 1]
).

Definition 3.2
.

The
magma operad

\Omega=\{\Omega(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
is the free operad in
\mathbf{Set}
generated by a binary operation
\mu(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}x_{2}\in\Omega(2)
. As such, the set
\Omega(n)
is the set of all planar, binary, rooted trees with
n
leaves, labelled by
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Here, the term binary means that every vertex in our rooted tree has two inputs and one output. Operadic composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(n)\times\Omega(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(n+m-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}

is given by grafting the root of a tree
T^{\prime}\in\Omega(m)
to the
i
th leaf of a tree
T\in\Omega(n)
. See
[
26
, Section 6.1.1]
for more details.

Elements of
\Omega(n)
are equivalently described as maximally parenthesized permutations in the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For example
(12)3
and
3(12)
are two elements of
\Omega(3)
. There’s a natural map
\omega:\Omega(n)\rightarrow\Sigma_{n}
which forgets the parenthesization of a permutation.

Definition 3.3
.

For
n\geq 1
, we define a sequence of categories
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
with:

•

\operatorname{ob}(\mathsf{PaRB}(n))=\Omega(n)
;

•

morphisms
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(n)}(p_{1},p_{2}):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{CoRB}(n)}(\omega(p_{1}),\omega(p_{2}))
are elements of the ribbon braid group
\mathsf{RB}_{n}
whose underlying permutation is
\omega(p_{2})\omega(p_{1})^{-1}
.

The collection
\mathsf{PaRB}=\{\mathsf{PaRB}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
forms an operad in groupoids where composition functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PaRB}(n)\times\mathsf{PaRB}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PaRB}(n+m-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}

are defined on objects as in the operad
\Omega
. On morphisms, the operad composition is defined as in
\mathsf{CoRB}
.

\beta:=\begin{array}[]{c}{\hbox to40.82pt{\vbox to70.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-50.36768pt\lower-56.34755pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

Figure 3.
Generating morphisms braiding
\beta
, associator
\alpha
and twist
\tau
of
\mathsf{PaRB}

At the level of objects, the operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
is the free operad in sets generated by a single binary operation
\mu\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathsf{PaRB}(2))
(Definition
3.2
).
Morphisms in
\mathsf{PaRB}
are generated under operadic and categorical composition by the ribbon braid generators

\beta\in\mathsf{B}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\in\mathsf{RB}_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z},

viewed as isomorphisms

\beta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(2)}(12,21)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(1)}(1,1),

together with an additional isomorphism

\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(3)}((12)3,\,1(23)),

called the
associator
. Because the operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
admits a finite presentation, we can classify maps out of
\mathsf{PaRB}
with a particularly simple description, summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
13
, Lemma 7.4]
,
[
26
, Theorem 6.2.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be any operad in groupoids. There is a bijection between the set of operad maps
f:\mathsf{PaRB}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
and the set of values

f(\mu)=\mathtt{m}\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}(2)),\quad f(\tau)=\mathtt{t}\in\mathcal{Q}(1),\quad f(\beta)=\mathtt{b}\in\mathcal{Q}(2)\quad\text{and}\quad f(\alpha)=\mathtt{a}\in\mathcal{Q}(3)

which satisfy the following equations:

(T)

(\mathtt{t}\circ_{1}1_{\mathtt{m}})=\mathtt{b}\cdot(21)^{*}\mathtt{b}\cdot(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ(\mathtt{t},\mathtt{t}))\in\mathcal{Q}(2).

(H1)

(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ_{1}\mathtt{b})\cdot((213)^{*}\mathtt{a})\cdot(213)^{*}(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ_{2}\mathtt{b})=\mathtt{a}\cdot(\mathtt{b}\circ_{2}1_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(231)^{*}\mathtt{a}\in\mathcal{Q}(3).

(H2)

(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ_{2}\mathtt{b})\cdot(132)^{*}\mathtt{a}^{-1}\cdot(132)^{*}(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ_{1}\mathtt{b})=\mathtt{a}^{-1}\cdot(\mathtt{b}\circ_{1}1_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(312)^{*}\mathtt{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{Q}(3).

(P)

(\mathtt{a}\circ_{1}1_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(\mathtt{a}\circ_{3}1_{\mathtt{m}})=(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ_{1}\mathtt{a})\cdot(\mathtt{a}\circ_{2}1_{\mathtt{m}})\cdot(1_{\mathtt{m}}\circ_{2}\mathtt{a})\in\mathcal{Q}(4).

∎

Remark 3.5
.

The operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
can be equivalently defined as an operadic pullback of
\mathsf{CoRB}
along the map
\omega:\Omega\rightarrow\operatorname{ob}(\mathsf{CoRB})
, i.e.
\mathsf{PaRB}=\omega^{*}(\mathsf{CoRB})
. Moreover, we have a zigzag of equivalences:

\mathsf{PaRB}\underset{}{\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{CoRB}{\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}_{2}),

where
\mathsf{PaRB}
is a cofibrant object in
\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{G})

[
13
, Corollary 6.14]
,
[
66
]
.

The defining relations in Theorem
3.4
coincide with the coherence relations of balanced monoidal categories introduced in Section
1.1
. This is not a coincidence, as summarized in the following theorem of Wahl:

Theorem 3.6

(
[
66
, Theorem II.5.11]
)
.

Algebras over the operad of coloured ribbon braids
\mathsf{CoRB}
are precisely strict balanced monoidal categories. Algebras over the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids
\mathsf{PaRB}
are balanced monoidal categories.

3.3.
The cyclic operad of ribbon braids

The cyclic structure of
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
induces, via the fundamental groupoid functor, cyclic structures on the operads
\mathsf{CoRB}

[
57
, Proposition 5.3]
and
\mathsf{PaRB}

[
16
, §3.1]
. While these cyclic structures have been described previously, we include here a detailed verification of the cyclic operad axioms, as some of these details are required for later constructions.

Because the operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
admits a finite presentation, it is sufficient to define the cyclic structure on the generating morphisms as

(3.1)

\displaystyle z_{2}^{*}(\tau)=\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(1)}(1,1),\quad z_{3}^{*}(\beta)=(\beta^{-1})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(2)}(12,21),

(3.2)

\displaystyle z_{4}^{*}(\alpha)=(231)^{*}\alpha^{-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(3)}((12)3,1(23)).

The cyclic action
z^{*}
on the braiding
\beta
, associator
\alpha
and twist
\tau
, of the generating morphisms of
\mathsf{PaRB}
can be pictorially represented as follows:

Here, the symbol
on a ribbon strand represents the positive twist and
represents a negative twist. Note that the cyclic action on the braiding
\beta:\mu(x_{1},x_{2})\longrightarrow\mu(x_{2},x_{1})
determines the action on
\beta^{-1}
,
(21)^{*}\beta
and
(21)^{*}\beta^{-1}
as in
[
57
, §5]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The cyclic action
z^{*}_{4}
on the associator
\alpha:\mu\circ_{1}\mu\longrightarrow\mu\circ_{2}\mu
is a
\Sigma_{4}
-action that extends the
\Sigma_{3}
-action on
\alpha
coming from the operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
.

Proof.

We rewrite the action
z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha=(231)^{*}\alpha^{-1}
as
z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha_{1,2,3}=\alpha_{2,3,1}^{-1}
to facilitate computation and notation. The action
z^{*}_{4}

Similarly, we have
z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha^{-1}=(231)^{*}\alpha
and
(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1}=z^{*}_{4}((231)^{*}\alpha^{-1})=\alpha^{-1}
, therefore
(z^{*}_{4})^{4}\cdot\alpha=\alpha
. We denote
\sigma_{2,1}
for the transposition
(21)^{*}
. To check the cyclic action is compatible with the symmetric group action, we first check that the following relations in
\Sigma_{4}
applied on
\alpha
holds:

(1)

(z^{*}_{4})^{4}=1
.

(2)

z^{*}_{4}\sigma_{2,1}z_{4}\sigma_{2,1}=\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{3}
.

(3)

\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}=(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\sigma_{2,1}
.

The relation (
1
) holds due to
{z^{*}_{4}}^{2}\cdot\alpha=\alpha
. For (
2
),
z^{*}_{4}\sigma_{2,1}\alpha=z^{*}_{4}(\alpha_{2,1,3})=\alpha_{3,1,2}
and then,

z^{*}_{4}\sigma_{2,1}\alpha_{3,1,2}=z^{*}_{4}(\alpha_{3,2,1})=\alpha_{1,3,2}^{-1}

and,
\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{3}(\alpha)=\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})(\alpha)=\sigma_{2,1}(\alpha_{2,3,1}^{-1})=\alpha_{1,3,2}^{-1}
.
For the last relation (
3
), we use the action
z^{*}_{4}(\alpha_{3,1,2})=\alpha_{2,1,3}
. Now, one can easily see the action
\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}(\alpha)=\alpha_{2,1,3}
, and we finally have,

\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}(\alpha_{2,1,3})=\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})(\alpha_{3,1,2})=\sigma_{2,1}(\alpha_{2,1,3})=\alpha_{1,2,3}

(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\sigma_{2,1}(\alpha)=(z^{*}_{4})^{2}\sigma_{2,1}(z^{*}_{4})^{2}(\alpha_{2,1,3})=\sigma_{2,1}(\alpha_{2,1,3})=\alpha_{1,2,3}

We need to check the relation
z_{3}^{*}\sigma_{2,1}=\sigma_{2,1}(z_{3}^{*})^{2}
from
\Sigma_{3}
on
\alpha
to claim that the action
z_{4}^{*}
is a
\Sigma_{4}
-action on associator that extends
\Sigma_{3}
-action:

z_{3}^{*}\sigma_{2,1}(\alpha)=z_{3}^{*}\alpha_{2,1,3}=\alpha_{3,2,1}.

\sigma_{2,1}(z_{3}^{*})^{2}(\alpha)=\sigma_{2,1}(z_{3}^{*})\alpha_{2,3,1}=\sigma_{2,1}\alpha_{3,1,2}=\alpha_{3,2,1}.

∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
z_{2}^{*}
denote the nontrivial element of
\Sigma_{1}^{+}
.
The assignments of
z_{2}^{*}
–actions on the generating morphisms specified in (
3.1
)
extend uniquely to a cyclic action of
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
on each
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
, thereby endowing
\mathsf{PaRB}
with the structure of a cyclic operad.

Proof.

The cyclic action on generators extends to the cyclic structure of
\mathsf{PaRB}
via the formula in (
2.1
). It is enough to check the compatibility with the pentagon Eq(
P
), the hexagons Eq(
H1
) and Eq(
H2
) taking place in
\mathsf{PaRB}
. The proof follows the strategy of
[
57
, Proposition 5.2]
, with parenthesization. The pentagon equation is given by

(P)

~(\alpha\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2})\cdot(\alpha\circ_{3}\operatorname{id}_{2})=(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\alpha)\cdot(\alpha\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\alpha)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}(((12)3)4,1(2(34))).

We first apply the cyclic action
z_{4}
on the morphism
(\alpha\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2}):(\mu\circ_{1}\mu)\circ_{1}\mu\longrightarrow\mu\circ(\mu,\mu)
, which amounts to applying the action on
(\mu\circ_{1}\mu)\circ_{1}\mu
and
\mu\circ(\mu,\mu)
then rewriting the resulting morphisms in terms of operadic composition of morphisms in
\mathsf{PaRB}
. We obtain
z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\alpha\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2})=(2341)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\alpha^{-1})
, similar to Figure
4
.
Similarly, one can compute the following action on the components of Eq(
P
);

\displaystyle z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\alpha\circ_{3}\operatorname{id}_{2})

\displaystyle=(2341)^{*}(\alpha^{-1}\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2}),

\displaystyle z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\alpha)

\displaystyle=(2341)^{*}(\alpha^{-1}\circ_{3}\operatorname{id}_{2}),

\displaystyle z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\alpha\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2})

\displaystyle=(2341)^{*}(\alpha^{-1}\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2}),

\displaystyle z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\alpha)

\displaystyle=(2341)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\alpha).

It is immediate from Figure
5
that that action is preserved when applied on Eq(
P
).

(H1)

\alpha\cdot(\beta\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2})\cdot(231)^{*}\alpha=(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)\cdot(213)^{*})\alpha\cdot(213)^{*})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}((12)3,2(31)).

(H2)

\alpha^{-1}\cdot(\beta\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2})\cdot(312)^{*}\alpha^{-1}=(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta)\cdot(132)^{*})\alpha^{-1}\cdot(132)^{*})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}(1(23),(31)2).

We use the relation
z^{*}(213)^{*}=(213)^{*}(z^{*})^{2}
to compute the action applied on the left side of Eq(
H1
) reduces to the following:

(z_{4}^{*}\cdot\alpha)\cdot(z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\beta\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2}))\cdot(z_{4}^{*}\cdot((231)^{*}\alpha))=(231)^{*}(\alpha^{-1}\cdot(\beta^{-1}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\tau^{-1})\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2})\cdot\alpha)

It can be written equivalent to
(231)^{*}\beta_{2}^{-1}\beta_{1}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\tau_{3}^{-1}
as an element of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}(2(31),1(23))
.

(213)^{*}((z_{4}^{*})^{2}\cdot((\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta))
gives

(3.3)

\begin{split}((213)^{*}z_{4}^{*}\cdot((231)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta))&=(312)^{*}\beta_{1}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}\tau_{3}^{-1}.\end{split}

Using the last equation, the right side of Eq(
H1
) is the straightforward, and it is given by

z_{4}^{*}\cdot((\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)\cdot(213)^{*}\alpha\cdot(213)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta))=(231)^{*}\beta_{1}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}\tau_{3}^{-1}\beta_{1}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}\tau_{3}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}(2(31),1(23)).

Similarly, we obtain the left side of Eq(
H2
) as follows

\begin{split}(z_{4}^{*}\cdot\alpha^{-1})\cdot(z_{4}^{*}\cdot(\beta\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{2}))\cdot(z_{4}^{*}\cdot(312)^{*}\alpha^{-1})&=(231)^{*}\alpha\cdot(231)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}(\beta^{-1}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1}))\cdot(213)^{*}\alpha^{-1}.\end{split}

It is equivalent to
(231)^{*}\beta_{2}^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}((23)1,(21)3)
. Using the relation
z_{4}^{*}(132)^{*}=(213)^{*}z_{4}^{*}
, the right side of Eq(
H2
) reduces to

\begin{split}z_{4}^{*}\cdot((\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta)\cdot(132)^{*}\alpha^{-1}\cdot(132)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta))&=(231)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)\cdot(321)^{*}\alpha\cdot(321)^{*}\left(\beta^{-1}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1})\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2}\right)\end{split}

which, is equivalent to
(231)^{*}\beta_{1}\beta_{1}^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}((23)1,(21)3)
.
Finally, the relation Eq(
H2
) upon the cyclic action can be translated as below, identifying both sides.

At last, the cyclic compatibility with the ribbon relation
\tau\circ\operatorname{id}_{2}=\beta\cdot(21)^{*}\beta\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\tau)\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau)
in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}}((12),(12))
follows immediate from
[
57
, Lemma 5.1]
.

∎

It was shown in
[
57
, Proposition 5.3]
that there is an equivalence of cyclic operads
\mathsf{CoRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathsf{S}_{0})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
(Example
2.6
). The cyclic actions on
\mathsf{CoRB}
and
\mathsf{PaRB}
agree on all generators except the associator, however, Lemma
3.8
shows that the cyclic action on
\mathsf{CoRB}
lifts along the pullback defining
\mathsf{PaRB}
, i.e.

w^{*}(z^{*}\cdot\mathsf{CoRB})\simeq z^{*}\cdot(w^{*}\mathsf{CoRB}).

The category of cyclic operads in groupoids admits a simplicial model category structure in which a map
f:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
is a weak equivalence if
f_{n}:\mathcal{P}(n)\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}(n)
is an equivalence of groupoids for all
n\geq 0
(Proposition
4.7
).

Lemma 3.9
.

The cyclic operad
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is cofibrant in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
.
Moreover, there are weak equivalences

\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{CoRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}}),

so that
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is a cofibrant replacement for
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
.

Proof.

For each
n\geq 1
, the functor
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)\to\mathsf{CoRB}(n)
, which forgets parenthesization on objects, is an equivalence of groupoids. The cyclic structure we impose on
\mathsf{PaRB}
enlarges the
\Sigma_{n}
–action to a
\Sigma_{n+1}
–action by permuting the output, but it does not add
new morphisms in arity
n
. Hence the induced functor

\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)\longrightarrow\mathsf{CoRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)

is also an equivalence for all
n\geq 1
, and the composite
\mathsf{CoRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)\to\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n))
is an equivalence by Proposition 5.3 of
[
57
]
.

For cofibrancy, observe that the underlying cyclic operad of objects
\operatorname{ob}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
is a free cyclic operad in sets (generated by the single binary operation
\mu
together with its cyclic symmetries). By Corollary
4.11
, it follows that
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is cofibrant in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
.

Combining these facts yields weak equivalences

\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{CoRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}}),

so
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is a cofibrant replacement of
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{FD}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
.
∎

4.
Cyclic infinity operads

Working with profinite completions of operads often requires a model in which operadic composition is associative only up to coherent homotopy—that is, a model for
\infty
–operads. For concreteness, we adopt the dendroidal Segal space framework for both
\infty
–operads
[
17
]
and cyclic
\infty
–operads
[
35
,
19
]
. In this section, we briefly review the combinatorics of trees, the construction of free (cyclic) operads (Section
4.1
), and some basic theory of
\infty
–operads and cyclic
\infty
–operads (Sections
4.4
and
4.5
). We conclude by constructing a cyclic
\infty
–operad of framed configurations (Section
4.6
), which provides a homotopical approximation to a cyclic
\infty
–operad of framed discs in profinite spaces, as described in Section
4.8
.

4.1.
Trees and the homotopy theory of (cyclic) operads

We begin by recalling the combinatorics of planar rooted and unrooted trees, which provide the basic indexing objects for operads and cyclic operads, respectively. A
graph

G
is a tuple
(V(G),H(G),s,i)
consisting of a set of vertices
V(G)
, a set of half–edges
H(G)
, a source map
s\colon H(G)\to V(G)
, and an involution
i\colon H(G)\to H(G)
. The orbits of the involution
i
are the
edges
of
G
, denoted
E(G)
. An edge
\{h,i(h)\}
with
i(h)\neq h
is an
internal edge
; the set of internal edges is denoted
\mathrm{iE}(G)
. Fixed points of
i
correspond to
external edges
, or
boundary edges
, and the set of all external edges is denoted
\partial(G)
.

Definition 4.1
.

A
tree
is a simply connected graph. We distinguish between two related types of trees:

•

A
rooted tree
is a tree equipped with a distinguished external edge
r\in\partial(T)
called the
root
.
The remaining external edges
\partial(T)\setminus\{r(T)\}
are the
leaves
, denoted
l(T)
.

•

An
unrooted tree
is a tree with no distinguished external edge; the entire set
\partial(T)
is viewed as its
boundary
.

A (rooted or unrooted) tree
S
is a
subtree
of
T
if
V(S)\subseteq V(T)
,
H(S)\subseteq H(T)
, and the structure maps for
S
are restrictions of the structure maps for
T
, defining
i(h)=h
in
S
if
i(h)=h^{\prime}
in
T
with
h^{\prime}\notin H(S)
, so that for every
v\in V(S)
, the arity of
v
in
S
and
T
is the same.

Definition 4.2
.

A
planar tree
is a tree endowed with an embedding into the plane, which induces for each vertex
v\in V(T)
a cyclic ordering of the half edges incident to
v
. A
labelled
planar (unrooted) tree is a triple
(T,\sigma,\tau)
, where
T
is a planar tree equipped with bijections

\sigma:\{1,\dots,|V(T)|\}\xrightarrow{\cong}V(T)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau:\{0,1,\dots,|\partial(T)|-1\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(T).

Two such triples
(T,\sigma,\tau)
and
(T^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
are
isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism of planar trees
T\to T^{\prime}
that respects the vertex– and boundary–labelings
\sigma,\tau
. A non-planar isomorphism of trees permutes the labelings.

Examples of trees include the degenerate tree
\eta=|
with no vertices and a single edge, and the
n
–corolla
,
C_{n+1}
which is the unrooted tree with one vertex and
n+1
leaves labelled by the set
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
. We will also write
C_{n}
for the rooted corolla.

Labelled trees capture the combinatorics of cyclic operads. In particular, there exists an
\mathbb{N}
-coloured operad whose algebras are precisely the one-coloured cyclic operads.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a non-empty set of colours. A
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured operad
in
\mathbf{E}
consists of a collection of objects
\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
in
\mathbf{E}
indexed by finite lists of colours
(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
in
\mathfrak{C}
, each equipped with a right action of the symmetric group

\sigma^{*}\colon\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,c_{\sigma(n)}),\qquad(\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}),

together with partial composition maps

\circ_{i}\colon\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\times\mathcal{P}(d_{0};d_{1},\ldots,d_{j})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\ldots,d_{j},c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{n}),

defined whenever
c_{i}=d_{0}
, and unit elements
\iota_{c}\in\mathcal{P}(c;c)
for each colour
c\in\mathfrak{C}
,
subject to the usual associativity, unit, and equivariance axioms (see
[
10
, Definition 1.1]
for details).

Remark 4.4
.

When the color set is
\mathfrak{C}=\{*\}
, a
\mathfrak{C}
–colored operad is a one-colored operad.

An
algebra
over a (
\mathfrak{C}
–colored) operad
\mathcal{P}
is a collection of objects
\{X(c)\}_{c\in\mathfrak{C}}
in
\mathbf{E}
together with evaluation maps

\rho\colon\mathcal{P}(c;c_{1},\dots,c_{k})\times X(c_{1})\times\dots\times X(c_{k})\longrightarrow X(c)

satisfying appropriate associativity, unit and equivariance conditions, see e.g.
[
10
, Definition 1.2]
. The category of
\mathcal{P}
-algebras in
\mathbf{E}
is denoted
\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathcal{P})
.

Example 4.5
.

The
operad for cyclic operads

\mathbf{C}
is the
\mathbb{N}
–coloured operad whose operations

\mathbf{C}(n;m_{1},\dots,m_{k})

are the set of isomorphism classes of labelled planar unrooted trees
(T,\sigma,\tau)
. The symmetric group
\Sigma_{k}
acts on
\mathbf{C}(n;m_{1},\dots,m_{k})
by permuting the vertex labels via
\sigma
.

For
(T,\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbf{C}(n;m_{1},\dots,m_{k})
and
(T^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\in\mathbf{C}(m_{i};b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})
,
the partial composition

\circ_{i}\colon\mathbf{C}(n;m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\times\mathbf{C}(m_{i};b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}(n;m_{1},\dots,b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell},\dots,m_{k})

is defined by
boundary-labelled tree substitution
:

(T,\sigma,\tau)\circ_{i}(T^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}):=T\bullet_{\sigma(i),\tau^{\prime}}T^{\prime}

where
T\bullet_{\sigma(i),\tau^{\prime}}T^{\prime}
is obtained by:

(1)

applying the permutation encoded by
\tau^{\prime}
to the
m_{i}
incident half-edges at the vertex
\sigma(i)\in V(T)
, and

(2)

gluing the boundary edges of
T^{\prime}
to these incident half-edges in the resulting order, respecting the planar embedding.

The vertex labelling of the new tree is given by the composite bijection

\sigma\circ_{i}\sigma^{\prime}\colon\{1,\dots,k+\ell-1\}\xrightarrow{\cong}V\bigl(T\bullet_{\sigma(i),\tau^{\prime}}T^{\prime}\bigr),

and its boundary labelling by the induced map

\tau\circ_{i}\tau^{\prime}\colon\{0,1,\dots,n+m_{i}-2\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial\bigl(T\bullet_{\sigma(i),\tau^{\prime}}T^{\prime}\bigr).

1
2
3
4
1
2
3
4
5
6
\circ_{4}
=
5
8
1
2
3
4
7
0
0
0

Figure 6.
For trees
(T,\sigma,\tau)
with
\sigma:\{1\}\rightarrow V(T)
,
\tau:\{0,1,\ldots,5\}\rightarrow\partial(T)
and
(T^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
with
\sigma^{\prime}:\{1\}\rightarrow V(T^{\prime})
,
\tau^{\prime}:\{0,1,\ldots,4\}\rightarrow\partial(T^{\prime})
, their composition in arity 4 is given by
(T,\sigma,\tau)\circ_{4}(T^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

There is a corresponding
\mathbb{N}
–coloured operad governing operads, which we denote by
\mathbf{O}
.
Its operations

\mathbf{O}(n;m_{1},\dots,m_{k})

are isomorphism classes of labelled planar
rooted
trees
(T,\sigma,\tau)
, where the root of
T
is the unique outgoing edge.
Composition is given by root–preserving tree substitution. A full description appears in
[
10
, Example 1.56]
and
[
7
, Def. 2.9]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
denote the
\mathbb{N}
–colored operad for cyclic operads and
\mathbf{O}
the
\mathbb{N}
–colored operad for rooted operads.
Then, for any symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{E}
, there are natural isomorphisms of categories

\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{C})\cong\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{O})\cong\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E}).

When
\mathbf{E}
is a sufficiently well-behaved monoidal model category, the categories of algebras of the operads
\mathbf{O}
and
\mathbf{C}
inherit a model category structure from
\mathbf{E}
. In particular, Lemma
4.6
together with
[
10
, Thm. 2.1]
yields the following result.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a cofibrantly generated symmetric monoidal model category with cofibrant unit, a symmetric monoidal fibrant replacement functor, and a cocommutative coalgebra interval.
Then both categories

\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})=\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{O})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})=\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{C})

admit cofibrantly generated model category structures in which a morphism

f\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}

is a weak equivalence (respectively, fibration) if and only if each component

f_{n}\colon\mathcal{P}(n)\to\mathcal{Q}(n)

is a weak equivalence (respectively, fibration) in
\mathbf{E}
. ∎

Remark 4.8
.

Throughout this paper we consider three main examples of
\mathbf{E}
:

•

the category of simplicial sets
\mathbf{S}
with the Kan–Quillen model structure,

•

the category of groupoids
\mathbf{G}
with the standard model structure
[
1
]
, and

•

the category of profinite groupoids
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
equipped with the cofibrantly generated model structure of
[
43
, Thm. 4.12]
.

Each of these satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
4.7
, so both
\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})
and
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})
inherit the corresponding model structures.

4.1.1.
Adjunctions between operads and cyclic operads

Every planar unrooted tree
T
can be viewed as a rooted tree by choosing a distinguished boundary edge
r\in\partial(T)
and orienting all internal edges towards
r
. This choice of
root
singles out a canonical direction of flow on the tree and determines a partial order on its edges, with the root regarded as the minimal element. Conversely, forgetting the chosen root of a planar rooted tree yields an unrooted tree. Informally, a
labelled rooted tree
may be described as a labelled unrooted tree
(T,\sigma,\tau)
together with a chosen root edge
r\in\partial(T)
. In diagrams, we therefore always depict the root by the boundary edge labelled
0
.

The operad for operads,
\mathbf{O}
(e.g.
[
10
, Example 1.56]
,
[
7
, Definition 2.9]
), as a sub-operad of
\mathbf{C}
. The inclusion of operads
u:\mathbf{O}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
induces a Quillen adjunction

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

The left adjoint
u_{!}:\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})
is the “cyclic envelope” of an operad. Full details on the Quillen adjunction (in this form) can be found in
[
22
]
. An explicit description of the left adjoint can be found in
[
21
, Section 3.1]
or
[
67
, Section 9]
.

4.1.2.
Change of base for operads and cyclic operads

A coloured operad
\mathcal{P}
is called
\Sigma
-
cofibrant
if it is cofibrant in the underlying category of colored symmetric sequences (e.g.
[
34
, Definition 4.7]
). As we will show below, algebras over
\Sigma
