-cofibrant operads are known to have many good homotopical properties. The operads which parameterise operads and cyclic operads are known to be
\Sigma
-cofibrant and, as such, one can show that a (nice enough) monoidal Quillen adjunction

(4.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}

induces a Quillen adjunction

(4.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathbf{C}).}

Moreover, this becomes a Quillen equivalences if the original adjunction (
4.2
) is a Quillen equivalence.

Lemma 4.9
.

The adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{S})}
B
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

is a Quillen equivalence. In particular, the classifying space functor is homotopically fully faithful, i.e. the map

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{B}\mathcal{P},\textnormal{B}\mathcal{Q})

is a weak equivalence of spaces for all
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\in\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G}).
∎

4.2.
Cofibrant cyclic operads in groupoids

In the model structure on
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})
, the fibrant objects are the cyclic operads which are entrywise fibrant. For example, since every groupoid is fibrant, every
\mathcal{O}\in\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
is fibrant. In general, cofibrant objects are harder to describe, but for cyclic operads in groupoids we have a nice characterisation.

Let

\operatorname{ob}\colon\mathbf{G}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}

be the functor taking a groupoid to its set of objects, with left and right adjoints

\operatorname{Disc}\dashv\operatorname{ob}\dashv\operatorname{Codisc}
.
These extend entrywise to adjunctions

\operatorname{Disc}\colon\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{Set})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G}):\operatorname{ob},\qquad\operatorname{ob}\colon\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{Set}):\operatorname{Codisc}.

Proposition 4.10
.

A morphism
g:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
is a cofibration if and only if
\operatorname{ob}(g)
has the left lifting property with respect to
maps that are entrywise surjective.

Proof.

The argument is identical to that of
[
43
, Prop. 6.8]
, so we only indicate the main steps. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the class of morphisms
g:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
such that the induced map on object-level cyclic operads

\operatorname{ob}(g)\colon\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q})

has the left lifting property with respect to all maps that are aritywise surjective on objects. It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under transfinite compositions, pushouts, and retracts, and contains the generating cofibrations.

Conversely, assume
g:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
lies in
\mathcal{C}
and let
p\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a trivial fibration in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
.
We must show that every commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

admits a diagonal lift
l\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{A}
making both triangles commute.

Since
p
is a trivial fibration, each
p_{n}\colon\mathcal{A}(n)\to\mathcal{B}(n)
is an equivalence of groupoids and surjective on objects.
By hypothesis,
\operatorname{ob}(g)
has the left lifting property with respect to aritywise surjective maps, so we may first define
the object component of
l
aritywise as a lift in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{B}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(l)}

Next, since fibrations in
\mathbf{G}
are fully faithful and surjective on objects,
each
p_{n}
is an isofibration, so we can lift morphisms in
\mathcal{Q}(n)
inductively along
p_{n}
to define
l_{n}\colon\mathcal{Q}(n)\to\mathcal{A}(n)
,
ensuring compatibility with the cyclic operad composition and symmetric group actions.
The resulting map
l\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{A}
is a morphism of cyclic operads satisfying
p\circ l=h
and
l\circ g=f
.

Therefore every map in
\mathcal{C}
has the left lifting property with respect to trivial fibrations, and hence every such map is a cofibration.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a cyclic operad in groupoids such that its object operad
\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})
is a free cyclic operad in sets, then
\mathcal{P}
is cofibrant in

\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.10
, it suffices to show that
\emptyset\to\mathcal{P}
has the LLP with respect to entrywise surjective maps in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{Set})
. Given a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{B}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ob}(l)}

with
\operatorname{ob}(p)
entrywise surjective. The assumption that
\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})
means the map
\operatorname{ob}(l)\colon\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})\to\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{A})
can be uniquely determined by looking at the pre-image of the elements in
\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{B})
which are in the image of
\operatorname{ob}(h)
.
∎

4.3.
Free (cyclic) operads generated by a tree

In order to model
\infty
-operads in the next section, we will make use of the fact that every planar rooted tree
T
freely generates a coloured operad, denoted
\Omega(T)
, whose structure is completely encoded by the combinatorics of
T
.

Definition 4.12
.

A rooted tree
T
is
\mathfrak{C}
-
coloured
if it is equipped with a map
\kappa:E(T)\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}
, to some non-empty set
\mathfrak{C}.
Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\}
,
c_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}
, be a collection of objects in
\mathbf{E}
in which each
\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
is equipped with a right action of
\Sigma_{n}
. A
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured, planar, rooted tree
T
is
decorated
by
\mathcal{P}
if each vertex
v\in V(T)
is labelled by an operation in

p_{v}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{out}(v);\mathrm{in}(v)),

where
\mathrm{out}(v)
is the colour of the outgoing edge of
v
and
\mathrm{in}(v)=\kappa(e_{1}),\ldots,\kappa(e_{k})
is the ordered list of colours of the incoming edges of
v
.

The
free operad
on
\mathcal{P}
is the
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured operad whose
n
-ary operations are the
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured,
\mathcal{P}
decorated, planar, rooted trees
T
of arity
n
with leaves labelled by a bijection
\tau:\{1,\dots,n\}\rightarrow l(T)
. In other words, for each list of colours
c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}
,

F(\mathcal{P})(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}):=\Big(\coprod\limits_{(T,\kappa,\tau)}\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{out}(v);\mathrm{in}(v))\Big)/\sim,

where
(T,\kappa,\tau)
runs over all isomorphism classes of leaf-labelled
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured planar trees with
n
leaves such that
\kappa(\tau(i))=c_{i}
,
\kappa(r)=c_{0}
. The symmetric group acts on
F(\mathcal{P})
by permuting the labels of the leaves, acting on
\tau
, and composition in
F(\mathcal{P})
is given by grafting of trees,
T\circ_{i}T^{\prime}
, which is defined by attaching the root of
T^{\prime}
to the
i
th leaf of
T
. For more details see
[
10
]
or
[
7
, Definition 2.3]
.

To each planar, rooted tree
T
we can define a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured symmetric sequence
X(T)
with
\mathfrak{C}=E(T)
the set of edges of
T
and

X(T)(e;e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=\begin{cases}\{v\}&\text{if }(e;e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=(\mathrm{out}(v);\mathrm{in}(v))\text{ for some }v\in V(T),\\[3.0pt]
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Here
\mathrm{out}(v)
denotes the unique edge of the rooted tree
T
directed away from
v
toward the root, and
\mathrm{in}(v)
is the (planar) ordered list of incoming edges at
v
. The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts by permuting the inputs of each vertex.

Definition 4.13
.

The
free operad generated by
T
is then defined as

\Omega(T):=F(X(T)),

where
F
denotes the free operad functor.
Concretely,
\Omega(T)
is the
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured operad with operations

\Omega(T)(e;e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=\begin{cases}\{S\}&\text{if }(e;e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=(r;l(S))\text{ for some subtree }S\subseteq T,\\[3.0pt]
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Here
r
denotes the root edge of
S
and
l(S)
its ordered list of leaves. Composition in
\Omega(T)
is given by grafting of subtrees and the symmetric group acts by permuting the leaf labels.

4.3.1.
The free cyclic operad generated by a tree

The notion of a
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured cyclic operad is less familiar in the literature than that of a coloured operad.
In this setting, the set of colours
\mathfrak{C}
is assumed to carry an involution
(-)^{\ast}\colon\mathfrak{C}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{C},
which assigns to each colour
c
its dual
c^{\ast}
and may be trivial. We suggest
[
21
]
or
[
19
]
for further background.

Definition 4.14
.

A
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured cyclic operad
in
\mathbf{E}
consists of a collection of objects
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\}
, in
\mathbf{E}
indexed by finite lists of colours
(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
in
\mathfrak{C}
, each equipped with a right action of the extended symmetric group

\sigma^{*}\colon\mathcal{P}(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(c_{\sigma(0)},c_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,c_{\sigma(n)}),

\sigma\in\Sigma_{n+1}
, together with partial composition maps

\circ_{i}^{j}\colon\mathcal{P}(c_{0},\ldots,c_{i},\ldots,c_{n})\times\mathcal{P}(d_{0},\ldots,d_{j},\ldots,d_{m})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\ldots,d_{j-1},d_{j+1},\ldots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{n}),

defined whenever
c_{i}=d_{j}
, subject to the usual associativity, unit, and equivariance axioms (see
[
19
, Definition 3.2]
).

As before, a planar tree
T
is said to be
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured
if it is equipped with an involutive map of sets

\kappa\colon H(T)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\}
be a symmetric sequence in
\mathbf{E}
, where each
\mathcal{P}(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
carries a right action of the extended symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
. A
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured planar tree
T
is
decorated
by
\mathcal{P}
if every vertex
v\in V(T)
is labelled by an operation

p_{v}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{nb}(v)),

where
\mathrm{nb}(v)=\big(\kappa(e_{0}),\kappa(e_{1}),\ldots,\kappa(e_{n})\big)
denotes the ordered list of colours of the edges incident to
v
.

Definition 4.15
.

The
free cyclic operad

F(\mathcal{P})
on
\mathcal{P}
is the
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured cyclic operad whose operations are
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured,
\mathcal{P}
–decorated planar trees
T
equipped with a boundary labelling

\tau\colon\{0,\dots,n\}\longrightarrow\partial(T).

Explicitly, for each tuple
c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\in\mathfrak{C}
, define

F(\mathcal{P})(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}):=\Bigg(\coprod_{(T,\kappa,\tau)}\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{nb}(v))\Bigg)\Big/\,\sim,

where
(T,\kappa,\tau)
ranges over all isomorphism classes of
\mathfrak{C}
–coloured planar trees satisfying
\kappa(\tau(i))=c_{i}
for each
i
. The equivalence relation
\sim
identifies two
\mathcal{P}
–decorated trees

(T,\kappa,\tau,(p_{v})_{v\in V(T)})\sim(T^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},(p^{\prime}_{w})_{w\in V(T^{\prime})})

whenever there exists a non-planar isomorphism of trees
\alpha\colon T\to T^{\prime}
such that

\kappa^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\kappa,\quad\alpha\circ\tau=\tau^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{v}(\alpha)\cdot p_{v}=p^{\prime}_{\alpha(v)},

where
\sigma_{v}(\alpha)\in\Sigma_{n}^{+}
is the permutation of
\mathrm{nb}(v)
induced by
\alpha
.

The extended symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
acts on an element
(T,\kappa,\tau,(p_{v})_{v\in V(T)})\in F(\mathcal{P})
by permuting the labels of the boundary edges of
T
.
Composition in
F(\mathcal{P})
is given by grafting of trees: for two trees
T
and
T^{\prime}
, the composite
T\circ_{i}^{\,j}T^{\prime}
is obtained by gluing the boundary edges
\tau(i)\in\partial(T)
and
\tau^{\prime}(j)\in\partial(T^{\prime})
whenever the corresponding colours are compatible.

To each planar tree
T
we can define a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured symmetric sequence
X(T)
with
\mathfrak{C}=E(T)
the set of edges of
T
and

X(T)(e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=\begin{cases}\{v\}&\text{if }(e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=(\mathrm{nb}(v))\text{ for some }v\in V(T),\\[3.0pt]
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
acts by permuting the inputs of each vertex.

Definition 4.16
.

The
free cyclic operad generated by
T
is then defined as

\Omega^{cyc}(T):=F(X(T)),

where
F
denotes the free cyclic operad functor.

4.4.
Infinity Operads

The
dendroidal category

\Omega
is the full subcategory of coloured operads whose objects are the free operads
\Omega(T)
generated by planar rooted trees
T
. Equivalently, the objects of
\Omega
are planar isomorphism classes of planar rooted trees, and morphisms are defined by

\operatorname{Hom}_{\Omega}(S,T)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Op}}\big(\Omega(S),\Omega(T)\big),

that is, by operad maps between the corresponding free operads.

Morphisms in
\Omega
can be decomposed into compositions of
elementary morphisms
: non-planar tree isomorphisms; inner face maps, outer face maps and degeneracies. Inner face maps are contractions of an internal edge
e\in\mathrm{iE}(T)
,
\partial_{e}\colon T/e\longrightarrow T,
where
T/e
is obtained from
T
by collapsing
e
to a point. Outer face maps are inclusions
\partial_{v}\colon T/v\longrightarrow T
which correspond to deleting the vertex
v
together with its adjacent outer edge. Degeneracies
s_{v}\colon T/v\longrightarrow T
correspond to deleting a vertex
v
with
|\mathrm{in}(v)|=1
. In the opposite category
\Omega^{\mathrm{op}}
, outer face maps correspond to inclusions of certain admissible subtrees, while inner face maps correspond to the contraction of internal edges. For detailed discussions and examples, see
[
55
,
56
,
40
]
.

Remark 4.17
.

Our definition of
\Omega
differs slightly from the standard one in that we take
planar
rooted trees as objects. Formally, this corresponds to the equivalent category
\Omega^{\prime}
described in
[
56
, Definition 3.1.1]
.

A
dendroidal object
in
\mathbf{E}
is a contravariant functor
X:\Omega^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}
. We write
X_{T}
for the evaluation of a functor
X
at a specific tree
T
. Informally, we think of the evaluation
X_{T}
as the set (or space) of decorations of the tree
T
by elements of
\mathbf{E}
. The category of dendroidal objects in
\mathbf{E}
, which we will denote by
\mathbf{dE}
, is the category whose objects are all functors
X:\Omega^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}
and whose morphisms are the natural transformations between them.

For each vertex
v
in a tree
T
there is an inclusion of a subtree,
C_{|\mathrm{in}(v)|}\hookrightarrow T
, which picks out the corolla around the vertex
v
in
T
. This subtree inclusions induces a map
X_{T}\rightarrow X_{C_{|\mathrm{in}(v)|}}
, for any given dendroidal object
X\in\mathbf{dE}
. Here, the notation
|\mathrm{in}(v)|
is meant to indicate the number of incoming edges of the vertex
v
in
T
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a monoidal model category. An
\infty
–operad
in
\mathbf{E}
is a dendroidal object
X:\Omega^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}
satisfying:

(1)

The value
X_{\eta}\simeq\ast
, where
\ast
is the terminal object of
\mathbf{E}
;

(2)

For every tree
T
, the
Segal map

X_{T}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V(T)}(X_{C_{|\mathrm{in}(v)|}})_{f}

is a weak equivalence in
\mathbf{E}
. Here
(\cdot)_{f}
denotes a fibrant replacement in
\mathbf{E}
.

The Segal condition ensures that
X
behaves like an operad “up to homotopy”. Informally, the evaluation of
X
at the edge
\eta
is the set of “colours” for the edges of our trees. The first condition
X_{\eta}\simeq\ast
is meant to indicate that we want to work with one-coloured operads. The evaluation at the corollas,
X_{C_{n}}
, denotes the set (or space) of
n
-ary operations and the Segal condition says that the value of
X
at
T
is determined by the homotopy‐product of the operations which decorate its vertices. The role of the fibrant replacement is to guarantee that the product is the homotopy product. Note that if
\mathbf{E}
is the category of spaces or groupoids, every finite product is a homotopy product and thus the fibrant replacements are not needed.

We will say that a map of dendroidal objects
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak equivalence if
X_{T}\rightarrow Y_{T}
is a weak equivalence in
\mathbf{E}
for all trees
T\in\Omega
. The
\infty
-category of
\infty
-operads in
\mathbf{E}
, denoted by
\mathbf{Op}_{\infty}(\mathbf{E})
, is the relative category whose objects are all
\infty
–operads in
\mathbf{E}
and whose weak equivalences are the entrywise weak equivalences of dendroidal objects.

An operad in
\mathbf{E}
can be regarded as an
\infty
-operad via a fully faithful nerve functor

\textnormal{N}:\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{dE}.

In particular, for any (one-coloured) operad
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathbf{E}
, its
dendroidal nerve

\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O}
is defined to be the set of maps

(\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O})_{T}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\Omega(T),\mathcal{O}).

In practice, the nerve of an operad
\mathcal{O}
at a tree
T
is the space of all “
T
-shaped” compositions of operations in
\mathcal{O}
, i.e.

(\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O})_{T}\,=\,\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{O}(|v|).

If
\mathcal{O}
is entrywise fibrant (or if finite products coincide with finite homotopy products in
\mathbf{E}
), then
\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O}
is an
\infty
–operad.

Remark 4.19
.

If
\mathbf{E}
is the category of spaces or of groupoids, the
\infty
–operads are precisely the fibrant objects in the left Bousfield localization of
\mathbf{dE}
at the Segal maps and at the map
\varnothing\to\Omega(-,\eta)
. See
[
17
]
for more details. When
\mathbf{E}
is the category of profinite spaces, however, the model structure is only fibrantly generated, which is why we prefer the
\infty
‐category formulation for discussing
\infty
-operads.

Not all
\infty
-operads come from taking the nerve of an operad. In particular, while configuration spaces do not form an operad, they do form an
\infty
-operad as illustrated in the following example.

Example 4.20
.

The ordered configuration spaces
\mathsf{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
do not form an operad, but one can approximate an operad of configuration spaces via the little discs operad
\mathsf{D}_{2}
since
\mathsf{D}_{2}(n)\simeq\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
for all
n\geq 0
. In
[
37
]
, Hackney shows that one can assemble configuration spaces into a non-unital version of an
\infty
–operad.

Definition 4.21
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be the category of simplicial sets. A
semi-dendroidal space
is a functor

X\colon\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}}^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{S},

where
\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}}\subseteq\Omega
is the wide subcategory of the dendroidal category generated by face maps and isomorphisms (i.e. morphisms that are injective on vertices).

A semi-dendroidal space
X
satisfies the
Segal condition
if, for every tree
T
, the canonical map

X_{T}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V(T)}X_{C_{|\mathrm{in}(v)|}}

is a weak equivalence. One can think of a semi-dendroidal space satisfying the Segal condition as an
\infty
-operad without units.

Hackney constructs a semi-dendroidal space
X
satisfying the Segal condition, whose corolla components recover the configuration spaces:

X_{C_{n}}\simeq\mathsf{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

In particular, the Segal maps
X_{T}\to\prod_{v\in V(T)}X_{C_{|v|}}
are weak equivalences, so the collection of configuration spaces
\{\mathsf{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\}_{n\geq 0}
determines a coherent, “up-to-homotopy”
operadic structure. Moreover, if we restrict the
\infty
-operad
\textnormal{N}\mathsf{D}_{2}:\Omega^{\text{op}}\rightarrow\mathbf{S}
to the subcategory
\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}}
, then we have a weak equivalence of semi-dendroidal spaces:

X\simeq\textnormal{N}\mathsf{D}_{2}.

Thus, the semi-dendroidal model
X
provides an explicit presentation of the non-degenerate part of the
\infty
–operad underlying
\mathsf{D}_{2}
in terms of configuration spaces.

4.5.
Cyclic infinity operads

Let
T
be a planar
unrooted
tree. As in the rooted case, we may associate to
T
the free cyclic operad generated by
T
, which we will denote by
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T)
.
The cyclic dendroidal category
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
4
4

4

This category was introduced under the notation
U_{\mathrm{cyc}}
in
[
35
]
and is denoted by
\Upsilon
in
[
19
]
.
is the category whose objects are unrooted planar trees. A morphism
S\to T
in
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is then defined to be a morphism of cyclic operads
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(S)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T)
(
[
35
]
;
[
19
, Definition 4.8]
).

As in
\Omega
, morphisms in
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
can be expressed as compositions of non-planar tree isomorphisms, inner face maps, outer face maps, and degeneracies. A
cyclic dendroidal object
in
\mathbf{E}
is a contravariant functor

X\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{E}.

We denote the corresponding functor category by
\mathbf{cd}\mathbf{E}
. The analogue of the Segal condition in this setting ensures that the evaluation of a functor
X
at an unrooted tree
T
is determined by the operations which decorate its vertices.

Definition 4.22
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a monoidal model category. A
cyclic
\infty
–operad
in
\mathbf{E}
is a cyclic dendroidal object

X
such that:

(1)

X_{\eta}
is weakly equivalent to the terminal object of
\mathbf{E}
;

(2)

For every unrooted tree
T
, the Segal map

X_{T}\,\longrightarrow\,\prod_{v\in V(T)}(X_{C_{|\mathrm{nb}(v)|}})_{f}

is a weak equivalence in
\mathbf{E}
.

The
\infty
–category of cyclic
\infty
–operads in
\mathbf{E}
, denoted
\mathbf{Cyc}_{\infty}(\mathbf{E})
, is defined as the relative category whose objects are cyclic dendroidal objects in
\mathbf{E}
satisfying the Segal condition. A morphism
X\to Y
of cyclic
\infty
–operads is a weak equivalence if, for every tree
T\in\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
, the map
X_{T}\to Y_{T}

is a weak equivalence in
\mathbf{E}
.

The construction of the dendroidal nerve extends verbatim to the cyclic setting to define a fully faithful functor
\textnormal{N}:\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{cdE}
. In particular, for a given cyclic operad
\mathcal{P}
the nerve of
\mathcal{P}
at a tree
T
is defined to be
(\textnormal{N}\mathcal{P})_{T}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{Cyc}(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T),\mathcal{P})
which, in practice, looks like

(\textnormal{N}\mathcal{P})_{T}\cong\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{nb}(v)),

where
V(T)
denotes the set of vertices of
T
. Note that if
\mathcal{P}
is entrywise fibrant or if
\mathbf{E}
is the category of spaces or groupoids, then
\textnormal{N}\mathcal{P}
is a cyclic
\infty
-operad.

4.6.
Properties of the (cyclic) dendroidal nerve

As we will describe more fully in the appendix, both the category of operads and the category of cyclic operads in
\mathbf{E}
admit a cofibrantly generated model category structure in which a map of (cyclic) operads
f:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is a weak equivalence (respectively, fibration) if
f(n):\mathcal{O}(n)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(n)
is a weak equivalence (respectively, fibration) in
\mathbf{E}
(Proposition
4.7
). The following theorem follows from the Quillen equivalences in Theorem 8.15 of
[
18
]
and Theorems B and D of
[
19
]
. A more explicit proof can also be found for the operad case in Theorem 4.3 of
[
13
]
and a more explicit proof for the cyclic case will appear in
[
8
]
.

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be either the category of spaces or of groupoids.
The (cyclic) dendroidal nerve functor is homotopically fully faithful, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O},\textnormal{N}\mathcal{P})}

is a weak equivalence of spaces for all
\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P}
in
\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})
or
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})
.

There is a canonical functor

f\colon\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}},

which forgets the choice of root of a rooted tree. More explicitly, if we write
(T,r)
to denote a tree with root
r
, then we define
f(T,r)=T
. This defines a functor, as one can show that
f
sends a morphism of operads
\Omega(T,r)\to\Omega(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
to a map of cyclic operads
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T)\to\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T^{\prime})
. As shown in
[
38
]
and
[
19
, Section 6]
, the induced map on functor categories admits both a left and a right adjoint:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{d}\mathbf{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{cd}\mathbf{E}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

This adjunction preserves Segal objects (
[
39
, Theorem 6.23]
) and is compatible with the adjunctions between cyclic operads and operads

(4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

In particular, given a rooted tree
(T,r)
in
\Omega
there is an isomorphism of cyclic operads
u_{!}\Omega(T,r)\cong\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T)
(cf. just after
[
19
, Definition 4.11]
). This implies that we have

(4.5)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}(T),\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\Omega(T,r),\mathcal{P})

for all trees
T\in\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
. It follows that the nerve of cyclic operad
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
and the nerve of its underlying operad are compatible in the sense that the following diagram of right adjoints:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{cdE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{dE}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}
N
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
N

commutes.

4.7.
A cyclic
\infty
-operad of framed configurations

In this brief subsection, we describe a topological cyclic
\infty
-operad
X:(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Top}
whose components model the configuration spaces of framed points in the complex plane, in the sense that
X_{C_{n+1}}\simeq\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 1
.

We will first generalise Hackney’s construction of an
\infty
-operad of configuration spaces (Example
4.20
;
[
37
, Example 1]
) by using a
2
–coloured operad “with shifts”. Intuitively, one can think of this operad with shifts a “homotopically relaxed” analogue of the Swiss–Cheese operad. The two colours parametrize embeddings of framed discs and points in the plane, but the operad is also equipped with “shift” maps which interpolate between disks and points by continuously collapsing a disk to its centre.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
\mathsf{PD}
be a topological operad with colour set
\{1,2\}
. For any ordered list of colors
\ell=(l_{1},\dots,l_{n})
we set

\mathsf{PD}(2;\ell)=\begin{cases}*&\text{if }\ell=2,\\[4.0pt]
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

When the output colour is
1
, set
\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell)
to be the space of configurations

(a_{1},\dots,a_{n};\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\times(S^{1})^{\times n}

satisfying:

•

if
l_{j}=1
, then
(a_{j},\theta_{j})
describes an affine embedding

a_{j}(x)=r_{j}e^{i\theta_{j}}x+c_{j},\qquad r_{j}>0,\ c_{j}\in\mathbb{C},

of the unit disc
D^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
, representing a disc of radius
r_{j}
, centred at
c_{j}
, with framing angle
\theta_{j}
;

•

if
l_{k}=2
, then
a_{k}=(c_{k},\theta_{k})
is a
framed point
, consisting of a point
c_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
and an angle
\theta_{k}\in S^{1}
;

•

the closed discs
a_{j}(\overline{D^{2}})
are pairwise disjoint and disjoint from all framed points
c_{k}
.

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
\mathsf{PD}(\ell;1)
by permuting inputs. Given two points
x\in\mathsf{PD}(\ell;1)
and
y\in\mathsf{PD}(\lambda;1)
with
l_{i}=1
, then we define the substitution
x\circ_{i}y
by
rescaling and rotating the configuration
y
according to the embedding
a_{i}
:

x\circ_{i}y=\big(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},\ a_{i}\circ a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{i}\circ a_{q}^{\prime},\ a_{i+1},\dots,a_{p}\big),

where each
a_{i}\circ a_{j}^{\prime}
is the affine map

(a_{k}\circ a_{j}^{\prime})(x)=r_{k}e^{i\theta_{k}}\big(r_{j}^{\prime}e^{i\theta_{j}^{\prime}}x+c_{j}^{\prime}\big)+c_{k}=(r_{k}r_{j}^{\prime})e^{i(\theta_{k}+\theta_{j}^{\prime})}x+\big(r_{k}e^{i\theta_{k}}c_{j}^{\prime}+c_{k}\big).

In other words, we compose as in the standard framed little discs operad.

The operad
\mathsf{PD}
is an
operad with shifts
: for each pair of colour strings
\ell=(l_{1},\dots,l_{n})
and
\ell^{\prime}=(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{n}^{\prime})
of equal length with
l_{i}\leq l_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
, there exists a continuous
shift map

s_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\colon\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell)\longrightarrow\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell^{\prime}),

acting trivially when
l_{i}=l_{i}^{\prime}=1
, and collapsing the
i
-th framed disc to its centre (retaining its framing) when
l_{i}=1
,
l_{i}^{\prime}=2
. Intuitively, these maps allow framed discs to degenerate continuously into framed points while preserving the operadic structure up to homotopy.

These shift maps are compatible with the operadic structure in the following way.

(1)

For each
\ell
, the map
s_{\ell,\ell}
is the identity on
\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell)
.

(2)

Let
\ell=(l_{1},\dots,l_{n})
and
\ell^{\prime}=(l^{\prime}_{1},\dots,l^{\prime}_{n})
be colour strings of length
n
, with
l_{i}\leq l_{i}^{\prime}
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Similarly, let
\lambda=(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})
and
\lambda^{\prime}=(m_{1}^{\prime},\dots,m_{k}^{\prime})
be two colour strings of length
k
with
m_{j}\leq m_{j}^{\prime}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
.
Then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell)\times\mathsf{PD}(1;\lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PD}(\ell\circ_{i}\lambda;1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PD}(\ell^{\prime};1)\times\mathsf{PD}(\lambda^{\prime};1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell^{\prime}\circ_{i}\lambda^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\times s_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\ell\circ_{i}\lambda,\,\ell^{\prime}\circ_{i}\lambda^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}

where
\ell\circ_{i}\lambda
denotes the usual concatenation of colour strings corresponding to substituting the inputs of
\lambda
into the
i
th entry of
\ell
, and likewise for
\ell^{\prime}\circ_{i}\lambda^{\prime}
. In other words, “shifting” colours before or after composition yields the same result.

(3)

The shift maps are compatible with this
\Sigma_{n}
action:

s_{\ell\sigma,\ell^{\prime}\sigma}\bigl(x\cdot\sigma\bigr)\,=\,s_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(x)\cdot\sigma.

In other words, applying a permutation to the inputs before or after the shift
yields the same result.

Lemma 4.25
.

Each shift map
s_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\colon\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell)\to\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell^{\prime})
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Let
\ell=(l_{1},\dots,l_{n})
and
\ell^{\prime}=(l^{\prime}_{1},\dots,l^{\prime}_{n})
be colour strings of length
n
, with
l_{i}\leq l_{i}^{\prime}
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
. The map
s_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:\mathsf{PD}(1;l_{1},\dots,l_{n})\rightarrow\mathsf{PD}(1;l^{\prime}_{1},\dots,l^{\prime}_{n})
sends a framed disk
(a_{j},\theta_{j})
in
D^{2}
to its center
(a_{j}(0),\theta_{j})
if
l_{j}<l_{j}^{\prime}
. This defines a deformation retraction betweeen the source and target spaces and thus
s_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

The dendroidal nerve of the coloured operad
\mathsf{PD}
encodes all ways of decorating a tree by operations and colours compatible with its input–output structure. For a tree
T
, the value of the nerve

\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{PD})_{T}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Op}}\bigl(\Omega(T),\mathsf{PD}\bigr),

is the space of operad maps from the free operad generated by
T
into
\mathsf{PD}
. Equivalently, a point of
\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{PD})_{T}
consists of:

•

a colouring of each edge
e\in E(T)
by an element of the colour set
\{1,2\}
of
\mathsf{PD}
. This is given by a chosen function
\kappa:E(T)\rightarrow\{1,2\}
as in Definition
4.12
.

•

For each vertex
v\in V(T)
, a chosen point

p_{v}\in\mathsf{PD}(c_{e_{0}};c_{e_{1}},\ldots,c_{e_{k}}),

which decorates a vertex
v
in
T
. Here, the edges adjacent to the vertex
v
are ordered by the planar structure of
T
, labelled by
\tau_{v}:\{0,1,\ldots,k\}\rightarrow\mathrm{v}
,
e_{0}
being the outgoing edge. The colouring
\kappa
then assigns to each edge a colour, i.e.
\kappa(e_{i})=c_{e_{i}}
. The decoration
p_{v}
will then be an embedding of discs and points which corresponds to this data.

Recall that the semi-dendroidal category
\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}}
contains only face maps (no degeneracies) and hence encodes the data of a non-unital
\infty
–operad.

Definition 4.26
.

We define a
semi-dendroidal space

X^{\mathrm{fr}}\colon(\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Top}
which locally models framed configuration spaces. Set
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\eta}=\ast
, and for any tree
T
in
\Omega
, let

X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}\subseteq\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{PD})_{T}

be the subspace consisting of operad maps that send the root and all internal edges of
T
to colour
1
, and the leaves to colour
2
.
Equivalently, a point of
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}
is a tuple

(p_{v_{1}},\ldots,p_{v_{k}})\in\prod_{v_{i}\in V(T)}\mathsf{PD}\bigl(\mathrm{out}(v_{i});\mathrm{in}(v_{i})\bigr),

where for each vertex
v_{i}
, the component

p_{v_{i}}\in\mathsf{PD}(1;\ell_{i})

is a configuration of framed points and discs in the plane determined by the ordered list

\ell_{i}=(l_{1},\ldots,l_{m_{i}})
of incoming edges to
v_{i}
, which are painted by the colours 1 and 2.
Thus
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}
parametrises compatible choices of such local configurations indexed by the vertices of
T
, where internal edges correspond to compositions along colour 1.

In particular, when
T
is the
n
–corolla
C_{n}
, the only vertex has
n
incoming edges of colour
2
, and we recover the framed configuration space

X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{C_{n}}\simeq\mathsf{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

Proposition 4.27
.

The functor
X^{\mathrm{fr}}\colon(\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}})^{op}\to\mathrm{Top}
is a semi-dendroidal space satisfying the Segal condition. Moreover, for each corolla
C_{n}
, there is a weak homotopy equivalence
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{C_{n}}\simeq\mathsf{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

The proof follows exactly as the proof
[
37
, Thm. 4]
, but we include a brief sketch from completeness. By definition
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\eta}=*
. To verify the Segal condition, we note that nerve of any operad satisfies the Segal condition and thus
\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PD}
satisfies the Segal condition, i.e. we have weak homotopy equivalences

\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PD}_{T}\simeq\prod_{v\in V(T)}\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PD}_{C_{|v|}}.

One can then use the shift maps to show that the restriction to the subspace
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}\subset\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PD}_{T}
is a weak homotopy equivalence
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}\simeq\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PD}_{T}
, for all
T\in\Omega
. It follows that the Segal map
X_{T}^{\mathrm{fr}}\to\prod_{v\in V(T)}X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{C_{|v|}}
is a weak equivalence.

Finally, as a corolla
C_{n}
has no internal edges, the evaluation
X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{C_{n}}\simeq\mathsf{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
∎

The dendroidal nerve
\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})\colon\Omega^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Top}
of the framed little disks operad defines a topological
\infty
–operad.
Restricting this functor to the subcategory
\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}}
yields a semi–dendroidal space

\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})^{\prime}\colon(\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Top},

which we may view as the non-unital part of
\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})
.

Corollary 4.28
.

There is a weak equivalence of semi–dendroidal spaces

\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})^{\prime}\ \simeq\ X^{\mathrm{fr}}.

In particular, as (non-unital)
\infty
–operads,
X^{\mathrm{fr}}
and
\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})
are weakly equivalent.

Proof.

We define a weak equivalence of semi–dendroidal spaces via the restriction of the projective model structure on dendroidal spaces to
\Omega_{\mathrm{inj}}
and thus a map
f\colon X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if and only if each component
X_{T}\to Y_{T}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

At each corolla we have

(\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})^{\prime})_{C_{n}}\simeq\mathsf{FD}_{2}(n)\simeq\mathsf{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\simeq X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{C_{n}}.

Both functors satisfy the Segal condition, so for any tree
T\in\Omega
we obtain weak equivalences

(\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})^{\prime})_{T}\simeq\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathsf{FD}_{2}(|v|)\simeq\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathsf{Conf}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{|v|}(\mathbb{C})\simeq X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}.

Thus
f_{T}\colon(\textnormal{N}(\mathsf{FD}_{2})^{\prime})_{T}\to X^{\mathrm{fr}}_{T}
is a weak equivalence for every
T
, proving the claim.
∎

Passing from the framed to the cyclic context, recall from Example
2.6
that
\mathsf{FD}_{2}
admits a cyclic enhancement via a weak equivalence of operads
\mathsf{FD}_{2}\simeq u^{*}\mathsf{S}_{0}
, where
\mathsf{S}_{0}
denotes the cyclic genus zero surface operad of
[
57
]
. The dendroidal nerve functor preserves this weak equivalence, giving a weak equivalence of
\infty
-operads
\textnormal{N}\mathsf{FD}_{2}\simeq\textnormal{N}u^{*}\mathsf{S}_{0}
.

Because of the compatibility between the cyclic and dendroidal nerve constructions and the adjunction between operads and cyclic operads, the cyclic
\infty
-operad
\textnormal{N}\mathsf{S}_{0}
thus provides a homotopical model for a cyclic operad of framed configuration spaces.

Theorem 4.29
.

The cyclic
\infty
-operad of genus zero surfaces
\textnormal{N}\mathsf{S}_{0}
provides a model for the cyclic
\infty
-operad of framed configuration spaces.
In particular, there are weak equivalences of semi-dendroidal spaces

\textnormal{N}u^{*}\mathsf{S}_{0}^{\prime}\simeq\textnormal{N}\mathsf{FD}_{2}^{\prime}\simeq X^{\mathrm{fr}}.

4.8.
Profinite completion

For any small category
\mathbf{E}
, the category of pro-objects in
\mathbf{E}
, denoted
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{E})
, is obtained by freely adjoining all cofiltered limits to
\mathbf{E}
. Equivalently, objects of
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{E})
are cofiltered diagrams
X\colon I\to\mathbf{E}
, and morphisms are given by

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{E})}(\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{Y_{j}\}_{j\in J})=\lim_{j\in J}\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{E}}(X_{i},Y_{j}).

If
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
is a full subcategory closed under finite limits, one defines the category of
pro-
\mathbf{C}
-objects

\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{C})\subseteq\operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{E})
and an adjunction

(4.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathbf{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{C})\colon|-|.}

The left adjoint
\widehat{(-)}
is the
pro-
\mathbf{C}
-completion
functor. When
\mathbf{E}
is a cofibrantly generated simplicial model category and
\mathbf{C}
satisfies suitable hypotheses (e.g. those of a
fibration test category

[
12
, Definition 5.1]
), the category
\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{C})
carries a fibrantly generated model structure and the adjunction (
4.6
) is a simplicial Quillen pair.

Example 4.30
.

Let
\mathbf{Set}
denote the category of sets and let
\mathbf{Fin}\subset\mathbf{Set}
be the full subcategory of finite sets.
A
profinite set
is a cofiltered limit of finite sets in
\mathbf{Set}
, that is, an object of the pro–category

\widehat{\mathbf{Set}}\ :=\ \operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{Fin}).

Equivalently, a profinite set is a compact, totally disconnected Hausdorff topological space—often called a
Stone space
in the literature. The category of
\widehat{\mathbf{Set}}
is cartesian closed, complete, and cocomplete (see Johnstone
[
46
, Chapter 5]
), one may define categories and operads enriched in profinite sets. Such a category
\mathbf{C}
consists of a set of objects
\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{C})
and, for each pair of objects a profinite set of morphisms
\mathbf{C}(x,y)
, together with continuous composition maps

\mathbf{C}(y,z)\times\mathbf{C}(x,y)\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbf{C}(x,z)

satisfying the usual associativity and unitality conditions.

Example 4.31
.

Let
\mathbf{Gr}
denote the category of discrete groups and
\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\mathbf{Gr}
its full subcategory of finite groups. The category of
profinite groups
is

\widehat{\mathbf{Gr}}:=\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathbf{Gr}}(\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathrm{fin}}),

and the associated adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathbf{Gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{Gr}}\colon|-|}

has as its left adjoint the classical profinite completion functor

\widehat{\mathsf{G}}:=\lim_{\mathsf{N}\triangleleft_{f}\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{N},

where the limit runs over all finite-index normal subgroups
\mathsf{N}
of
\mathsf{G}
.

Example 4.32
.

Let
X
be a connected, locally Noetherian scheme with geometric point
\bar{x}\to X
. The
étale fundamental group
of
X
is defined by

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}(X,\bar{x}):=\lim_{Y\to X}\operatorname{Aut}(Y/X),

where the limit ranges over finite étale covers
Y\to X
. Each group
\operatorname{Aut}(Y/X)
is finite, so
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}(X,\bar{x})
is a profinite group.

For each integer
n\geq 2
, the framed configuration space in the complex plane

\mathsf{Conf}_{n}:=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid z_{i}\neq z_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j\}

is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and its complex points form a
K(\pi,1)
–space
whose topological fundamental group is the pure braid group
\mathsf{PB}_{n}
. The étale fundamental group of the base change
\mathsf{Conf}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
identifies with
the profinite completion of
\mathsf{PB}_{n}
:

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}\big(\mathsf{Conf}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\big)\cong\widehat{\mathsf{PB}}_{n}.

Example 4.33
.

Work of Artin–Mazur
[
2
]
, Friedlander
[
27
]
, and others established that the étale homotopy type of a scheme can be identified as a pro-object in the category of spaces. In
[
58
, Theorem 1]
, Oda proved that there is a weak equivalence of pro-spaces between the étale homotopy type of the moduli stack of genus zero curves with
n+1
parameterized boundary components,
\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}
and the profinite completion of the classifying space of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{0,n+1}
:

\Pi^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1})\simeq\widehat{\textnormal{B}\Gamma}_{0,n+1}.

Using the identification
\Gamma_{0,n+1}\cong\mathsf{PRB}_{n}
, this equivalence may be rewritten as

\Pi^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1})\simeq\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PRB}}_{n},

relating the étale homotopy type of the genus–zero moduli stack to the profinite completion of the classifying space of the pure ribbon braid group.

The profinite completion of groups extends to groupoids. A groupoid
\mathbf{E}
is
finite
if it has finitely many morphisms (and hence finitely many objects). Writing
\mathbf{G}_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
for the full subcategory of finite groupoids, one defines the category of
profinite groupoids

\widehat{\mathbf{G}}:=\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{G}_{\mathrm{fin}}).

A profinite groupoid can be viewed as a cofiltered limit of finite quotients of a discrete groupoid, obtained by quotienting by congruence relations with finite sets of morphisms and objects. Every finite groupoid is trivially profinite, regarded as a constant pro-object.

The category of profinte groupoids
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
admits a cofibrantly generated model structure, and the adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathbf{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\colon|-|}

is a Quillen adjunction
[
43
, Theorem 4.12 and Proposition 4.22]
. Since the profinite completion functor is a left adjoint, it need not preserve limits. However, there is a convenient criterion describing when profinite completion does preserve finite products, which we recall below.

Proposition 4.34

(
[
43
, Proposition 4.23]
)
.

Let
P
and
Q
be groupoids with finitely many objects. Then the canonical map

\widehat{P\times Q}\longrightarrow\widehat{P}\times\widehat{Q}

is an isomorphism in
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
.

Proposition
4.34
allows us to define the profinite completion of a cyclic operad in groupoids by applying profinite completion “entry-wise.” More explicitly, for a cyclic operad
\mathcal{O}=\{\mathcal{O}(n)\}
in groupoids with each
\mathcal{O}(n)
finite, the
profinite completion

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}
is the cyclic operad in profinite groupoids defined by entrywise completion
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}=\{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(n)\}
. Composition operations defined by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(n)\times\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{O}(n)\times\mathcal{O}(m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{O}(n+m-1)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\circ_{i}}}

where the first map is the inverse of the product isomorphism from Proposition
4.34
. Thus,
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}
inherits a cyclic operad structure by completion of the composition and cyclic actions of
\mathcal{O}
.

4.8.1.
Profinite completion of cyclic operads in spaces

A space
X
is called
\pi
-finite
if, for every choice of basepoint
x_{0}
, each homotopy group
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
is finite, and there exists
n_{0}\geq 0
such that
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})=\ast
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. The full subcategory
\mathbf{S}_{\pi}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
of
\pi
-finite spaces is closed under finite limits. Following
[
12
, Theorem 5.2]
, we define the category of
profinite spaces
as the pro-category

\widehat{\mathbf{S}}:=\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathbf{S}}(\mathbf{S}_{\pi}).

Quick
[
61
, Theorem 2.12]
constructed a fibrantly generated model structure on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
in which the cofibrations are monomorphisms and the weak equivalences are those maps that induce isomorphisms on
\pi_{0}
,
\pi_{1}
, and on twisted cohomology with finite coefficients. The adjunction

(4.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}:\mathbf{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{S}}:|-|}

is a Quillen adjunction
[
61
, Proposition 2.28]
.

The classifying space functor extends naturally to the profinite setting and provides a bridge between profinite groupoids and profinite spaces. It sends finite groupoids to
\pi
-finite spaces and therefore induces a simplicial Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Pi}_{1}\colon\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\colon\widehat{\textnormal{B}}.}

Example 4.35
.

The étale homotopy type of a scheme can be regarded as a pro-object in the
\infty
–category of profinite spaces (
[
2
]
,
[
27
]
). By Theorem 1 of Oda
[
58
]
, there is a weak equivalence of pro-spaces

\Pi^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1})\simeq\widehat{\textnormal{B}\Gamma}_{0,n+1},

where
\Gamma_{0,n+1}
denotes the mapping class group of a sphere with
n{+}1
marked points. Using the identification
\Gamma_{0,n+1}\cong\mathsf{PRB}_{n}
,
we obtain

\Pi^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1})\simeq\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PRB}}_{n},

where
\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PRB}}_{n}
denotes the profinite completion of the classifying space of the pure ribbon braid group.

These functors fit into a commutative square of simplicial Quillen adjunctions (see
[
12
, Example 7.14]
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{S}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
B
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\textnormal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Pi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|}

Thus, the profinite completions of groupoids and simplicial sets are related compatibly by the classifying space and fundamental groupoid functors.

However, for a general groupoid
\mathbf{E}
, the canonical comparison map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathbf{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{B}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}}

need not be a weak equivalence in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.
To describe the cases where this holds, we recall a classical notion due to Serre.

Definition 4.36

(Good groups and groupoids)
.

A discrete group
\mathsf{G}
with profinite completion
\widehat{\mathsf{G}}
is called
good

if, for every finite abelian
\mathsf{G}
-module
\mathsf{M}
, the natural restriction map

H^{*}(\widehat{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{M})\longrightarrow H^{*}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{M})

is an isomorphism.
A groupoid
\mathbf{E}
with finitely many objects is called
good

if the automorphism group of each object is a good group.

By
[
43
, Proposition 5.9]
,
if
\mathbf{E}
is a good groupoid, then the comparison map

\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathbf{E}}\longrightarrow\textnormal{B}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}

is a weak equivalence in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.

Unlike the relatively mild requirements we put on groupoids in Proposition
4.34
, the profinite completion functor
\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathbf{S}\to\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
very
rarely preserves finite products. The best we can do is to apply Proposition 3.9 of
[
13
]
provides a sufficient condition ensuring product preservation up to weak equivalence.

Proposition 4.37

(
[
13
, Proposition 3.9]
)
.

If
X
and
Y
are connected simplicial sets with good homotopy groups,
then the canonical map

\widehat{X\times Y}\longrightarrow\widehat{X}\times\widehat{Y}

is a weak equivalence of profinite spaces.

Thus, in order to describe profinite completion of cyclic operads in spaces we will require the use of cyclic
\infty
-operads. Given a “nicely filtered” small category
\mathscr{K}
, Blom and Moerdijk
[
12
, Theorem 2.4]

show that the pro-
\mathbf{C}
–completion of a coskeletal diagram
X\in\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{E})

is the diagram
\widehat{X}\in\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{op}},\operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{C}))
, where
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
is the subcategory of finite objects (e.g. finite groupoids or
\pi
-finite spaces).

Both the dendroidal category
\Omega
and its cyclic variant
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
are generalized Reedy categories
[
35
, Theorem 4.10, Remark 4.17]
, and thus every diagram

X\in\operatorname{Fun}((\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{E})
is automatically coskeletal. This observation gives the following entrywise notion of profinite completion of a (cyclic) dendroidal object.

Definition 4.38
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
denote either
\mathbf{S}
or
\mathbf{G}
, and let
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
denote the subcategory of
\pi
-finite spaces or finite groupoids. For a (cyclic) dendroidal object
X\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{E}
,
its
profinite completion
is the functor

\widehat{X}\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pro}(\mathbf{C}),

defined by
T\longmapsto\widehat{X}_{T}.

The next sequence of lemmas shows that, for cyclic operads in groupoids, taking the nerve commutes with entrywise profinite completion: the cyclic dendroidal nerve of the completed operad agrees with the entrywise completion of the cyclic
\infty
-operad obtained via the nerve functor.

Lemma 4.39
.

Let
X\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{G}

be a cyclic
\infty
-operad such that
X_{C_{n+1}}
is a finite groupoid
for every corolla
C_{n+1}\in\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
.
Then the entrywise profinite completion

\widehat{X}\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\widehat{\mathbf{G}}

is again a cyclic
\infty
-operad.

Proof.

Profinite completion preserves terminal objects
and, by Proposition
4.34
,
commutes with finite products:

\widehat{\prod\limits_{v\in V(T)}X_{C_{|\mathrm{nb}(v)|}}}\cong\prod\limits_{v\in V(T)}\widehat{X}_{C_{|\mathrm{nb}(v)|}}.

Hence the image of the Segal map under
\widehat{(-)}

remains an isomorphism for all
T\in\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
,
and
\widehat{X}
satisfies the Segal condition.
∎

Lemma 4.40
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a cyclic operad in groupoids
such that each
\mathcal{O}(n)
has finitely many objects.
Then there is a natural isomorphism of cyclic
\infty
-operads

\widehat{\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O}}\cong\textnormal{N}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}).

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})_{\mathrm{fin}}\subset\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})

be the subcategory of cyclic operads in groupoids that are finite in each arity.
For such
\mathcal{O}
, both routes in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})_{\mathrm{fin}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{cd}(\mathbf{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{cd}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
N
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
N

yield the same cyclic dendroidal object up to natural isomorphism,
since profinite completion preserves finite products and terminal objects.
Explicitly, for each tree
T\in\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
,

(\widehat{\textnormal{N}\mathcal{O}})_{T}=\widehat{\prod_{v\in V(T)}\mathcal{O}(|v|)}\,\cong\,\prod_{v\in V(T)}\widehat{\mathcal{O}(|v|)}\,=\,(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathcal{O}})_{T}.

∎

5.
Grothendieck-Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}

The goal of this section is to establish an isomorphism between the
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
and the group of homotopy automorphisms of the profinite completion of the cyclic operad of parenthesized ribbon braids. We achieve this result in two steps. First, by identifying the classical
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
with the group of automorphisms of the profinite operad
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
in Section
5.1
. Second, by lifting this identification to homotopy automorphisms in Section
5.2
. As an result, we construct a non-trivial Galois action on a cyclic
\infty
–operad built out of configuration spaces in Section
5.3
.

Let
\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
denote the profinite completion of the free group
\mathsf{F}_{2}
on two generators
x
and
y
.
Given a word in the free group
f\in\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
and a continuous homomorphism

\gamma\colon\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}\to\widehat{\mathsf{G}}

into a profinite group
\widehat{\mathsf{G}}
, we write
f(a,b)
for the image
\gamma(f)
under the assignment
\gamma(x)=a
and
\gamma(y)=b
. All identities below are understood to hold in the appropriate profinite completions of the corresponding discrete groups.

Definition 5.1

(
[
20
]
)
.

The
profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller monoid

\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
consists of pairs

(\lambda,f)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}\times\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}

satisfying the following relations:

(I)

f(x,y)=f(y,x)
,

(II)

x^{\mu}f(x,y)y^{\mu}f(y,z)z^{\mu}f(z,x)=1

in
\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
, where
xyz=1
and
\lambda=2\mu+1
,

(III)

f(x_{12},x_{23})f(x_{12}x_{13},x_{24}x_{34})f(x_{23},x_{34})=f(x_{13}x_{23},x_{24})f(x_{12},x_{23}x_{24})

in
\widehat{\mathsf{PB}}_{4}
.

The group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}=\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}^{\times}
of invertible elements is called the
profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
.
The group law is given by

(\lambda_{1},f_{1})*(\lambda_{2},f_{2})=\left(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2},f_{1}\big(f_{2}(x,y)^{-1}x^{\lambda_{2}}f_{2}(x,y),y^{\lambda_{2}}\big)\cdot f_{2}(x,y)\right).

5.1.
Maps out of the cyclic operad of parenthesized ribbon braids

In this section, we construct a natural
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
action on the completed cyclic operad of parenthesized ribbon braids by identifying
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
with the group of object-fixing automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
.

Given any morphism of cyclic operads
f\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
,
we may restrict along the forgetful functor
u^{*}\colon\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})\to\mathbf{Op}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})

to obtain an underlying map of operads
u^{*}(f):=\bar{f}\colon\mathsf{PaRB}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
.
This defines a natural map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\mathsf{PaRB},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

The main observation of this section is that any object-fixing morphism
f\colon\mathsf{PaRB}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
can be lifted to map of cyclic operads. In what follows, we write
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P})\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P})
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P})\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P})
for the subsets of morphisms of operads and cyclic operads, respectively, that fix objects.

Lemma 5.2
.

The forgetful map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\mathsf{PaRB},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

is an isomorphism. That is, every object-fixing morphism of operads

\mathsf{PaRB}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
in
\mathbf{Op}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})

extends uniquely to an object-fixing morphism of cyclic operads

\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}

in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})
.

Proof.

A map
\bar{f}:\mathsf{PaRB}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
is determined by an integer
\lambda=1+2\mu
in
\mathbb{Z}
and an element of the profinite free group on two generators,
g(x_{12},x_{23})\in\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
. More precisely, by applying Theorem
3.4
,
\bar{f}
is equivalent to the assignment:

\bar{f}(\mu)=\mu,\quad\bar{f}(\tau)=\tau^{\lambda},\quad\bar{f}(\beta)=\beta^{\lambda},\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{f}(\alpha)=g(x_{12},x_{13})\cdot\alpha.

To lift
\bar{f}
to a map
f\in\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
, we must check that the map defined by
\bar{f}
commutes with the cyclic action.

Let’s start with the twist generator
\tau
. Since
z^{*}_{2}\cdot\tau=\tau
, we have

(5.1)

\bar{f}(z^{*}_{2}\cdot\tau)=\bar{f}(\tau)=\tau^{\lambda}.

To compute the effect of the cyclic action
z^{*}_{2}\cdot\tau^{\lambda}
, note that the extended symmetric group action on
\mathsf{PaRB}(1)
is a groupoid automorphism
\sigma^{*}:\mathsf{PaRB}(1)\rightarrow\mathsf{PaRB}(1)
. Thus:

\sigma^{*}(\tau^{\lambda})=\underbrace{\sigma^{*}(\tau)\sigma^{*}(\tau)\cdots\sigma^{*}(\tau)}_{\lambda}=(\sigma\cdot\tau)^{\lambda},

for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{1}^{+}
. It follows that

(5.2)

\bar{f}(z^{*}_{2}\cdot\tau)=z^{*}_{2}\cdot\tau.

A similar argument holds for the generator
\beta\in\mathsf{PaRB}(2)
, as

(5.3)

\bar{f}(z^{*}_{3}\cdot\beta)=\bar{f}((\beta^{-1})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1}))=\bar{f}(\beta)^{-1}\bar{f}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1})=(\beta^{-\lambda})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-\lambda})

and

(5.4)

z^{*}_{3}\cdot\bar{f}(\beta)=z^{*}_{3}\cdot\beta^{\lambda}=(z^{*}_{3}\cdot\beta)^{\lambda}=((\beta^{-1})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1}))^{\lambda}=(\beta^{-\lambda})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-\lambda}).

To look at the cyclic action on the associator
\alpha
, we recall that the pure braid group
\widehat{\mathsf{PB}}_{3}
is generated by
x_{12}=\beta_{1}^{2}
,
x_{23}=\beta_{2}^{2}
and
x_{13}=\beta_{2}\beta^{2}_{1}\beta_{2}^{-1}
where
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are the standard generators of the braid group
\mathsf{B}_{3}
. We can write these generators as operadic composites of the generating morphisms
\operatorname{id}_{2},\beta\in\mathsf{PaRB}(2)
, i.e.

\beta_{1}=\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{2}=\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta.

We first compute the cyclic action on
x_{12}
and
x_{23}
, we have

z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}=z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta^{2})=z^{*}_{4}\cdot\left((\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}(21)^{*}\beta)\right)

Using the axioms of a cyclic structure on its tree presentation, we have that

(5.5)

~z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)=(z^{*}_{3}\cdot\beta)\circ_{2}(z^{*}_{3}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{2})=(231)^{*}\left(\beta^{-1}(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1})\right)\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{2}.

(5.6)

~z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}(21)^{*}\beta)=(z^{*}_{3}\cdot(21)^{*}\beta)\circ_{2}(z^{*}_{3}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{2})=(312)^{*}\left(((21)^{*}\beta^{-1})(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1})\right)\circ_{2}(\operatorname{id}_{2}).

Note that
z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}\beta)\in\operatorname{Hom}(2(31),3(12))
, and
z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{1}(12)^{*}\beta)\in\operatorname{Hom}((31)2,2(31))
. Upon substituting Eq(
5.5
) and Eq(
5.6
) in
z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}
, we now have

(5.7)

z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}=(231)^{*}\tau_{1}^{-1}\beta_{1}^{-1}\beta_{2}^{-1}(312)^{*}\beta_{2}^{-1}\beta_{1}^{-1}\tau^{-1}_{1}=(231)^{*}\tau_{1}^{-2}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}^{2}\beta_{1})^{-1}.

Noting that the
z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}\in\operatorname{Hom}(2(31),2(31))
, we can also write Eq(
5.7
):

z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}=(231)^{*}\alpha^{-1}\tau_{1}^{-2}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}^{2}\beta_{1})^{-1}\alpha

In this way, the pure braid term
\tau_{1}^{-2}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}^{2}\beta_{1})^{-1}
is in
\operatorname{Hom}((12)3,(12)3)
. It follows that we can use relations in
\mathsf{PB}_{3}
to further reduce the morphism.
5
5

5

The group-theoretic identities used are word relations in
\mathsf{PB}_{3}
and
\mathsf{RB}_{3}
; hence they remain valid after passing to the profinite completions
\widehat{\mathsf{PB}}_{3}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{RB}}_{3}
.
In particular, since element
w=x_{12}x_{13}x_{23}=(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{3}
generates the center of
\mathsf{PB}_{3}
, and
wx_{23}^{-1}=x_{12}x_{13}=\beta_{1}^{2}\beta_{2}\beta_{1}^{2}\beta_{2}^{-1}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}^{2}\beta_{1}
we can rewrite
z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}
as:

(5.8)

z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12}=(231)^{*}\alpha^{-1}\tau_{1}^{-2}(x_{23}w^{-1})\alpha.

Similarly, we can compute

(5.9)

z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{23}=z^{*}_{4}\cdot\left(\alpha(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta^{2})\alpha^{-1}\right)=(z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha)(z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta^{2}))(z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha^{-1}).

Since
z^{*}_{4}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{2}\circ_{2}\beta^{2})=(231)^{*}x_{12}\quad\text{and}\quad z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha^{-1}=(231)^{*}\alpha
, Eq(
5.9
) reduces to

(5.10)

z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{23}=(231)^{*}\left(\alpha^{-1}x_{12}\alpha\right).

We can now put this all together to show
\bar{f}(z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha)=z^{*}_{4}\cdot\bar{f}(\alpha)
. In particular, the left-hand side becomes:

\bar{f}(z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha)=\bar{f}((231)^{*}\alpha^{-1})=(231)^{*}\left(\alpha^{-1}\cdot g(x_{12},x_{23})^{-1}\right)=(231)^{*}\left(\alpha^{-1}\cdot g(x_{23},x_{12})\right).

The right-hand side,
z^{*}_{4}\cdot\bar{f}(\alpha)
, becomes:

z^{*}_{4}\cdot\bar{f}(\alpha)=z^{*}_{4}\cdot(g(x_{12},x_{23}))\cdot(z^{*}_{4}\cdot\alpha)=g(z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{12},z^{*}_{4}\cdot x_{23})\cdot\left((231)^{*}\alpha^{-1}\right).

Substituting from Equations (
5.8
) and (
5.10
) and using the identity

g(h^{-1}x_{23}h,h^{-1}x_{12}h)=h^{-1}g(x_{23},x_{12})h

for any
h\in\widehat{\mathsf{B}}_{3}
, we get:

(231)^{*}\left(\alpha^{-1}\cdot g((\tau_{1}^{-2}w^{-1})x_{23},x_{12})\right).

Now, we know that for any
p,q
and
r
in
\widehat{\mathsf{RB}}_{3}
with
rp=pr
and
rq=qr
, we have
g(p,q)=g(rp,q)=g(p,rq)
. Moreover, since twists always commute with pure braids and
w^{-1}
is central, we know that
(\tau_{1}^{-2}w^{-1})
commutes with
x_{23}
and
x_{12}
, and thus the factor of
\tau_{1}^{-2}w^{-1}
disappears from the last equation, and we obtain

g((\tau_{1}^{-2}w^{-1})x_{23},x_{12})=g(x_{23},x_{12}),

as required.
∎

The following proposition combines results of
[
26
, Theorem 11.1.7]
and
[
13
, Proposition 7.3]
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(-)
denote the monoid of object-fixing endomorphisms in
\mathbf{Op}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})
.
There is an isomorphism of profinite monoids

\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaB}}_{\mathbb{K}})\cong\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}})\cong\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}.

That is, each object-fixing endomorphism of the operad
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
is determined by a pair
(\lambda,f)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}\times\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
through its action on the generating tuple
(\beta,\tau,\alpha)
:

(\beta,\tau,\alpha)\,\longmapsto\,(\beta^{\lambda},\,\tau^{\lambda},\,f(x_{12},x_{23})\cdot\alpha_{1,2,3}),

and every such assignment defines a unique endomorphism of
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
.

We now extend the previous identification to the cyclic setting.
The key point is that Lemma
5.2
tells us that the cyclic structure on
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
introduces no new constraints on object-fixing endomorphisms.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(-)
denote the monoid of object-fixing endomorphisms of a cyclic operad in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})
.
There is a natural isomorphism of profinite monoids

\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}.

Proof.

Because profinite completion is defined as a limit, any morphism
f\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
factors uniquely through
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

This induces an injective map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

To see that
\epsilon
is surjective, note that any endomorphism

\widetilde{f}\in\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})

restricts along
i
to a morphism

f=\widetilde{f}\circ i\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
,
and by construction
\epsilon(f)=\widetilde{f}
.

Now, Lemma
5.2
tells us that any object-fixing map of operads
\bar{f}:\mathsf{PaRB}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
uniquely lifts to a map of cyclic operads
f:\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
, giving an isomorphism

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\mathsf{PaRB},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}).

By the discussion above, we have
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
and Proposition
5.3
identifies the right-hand side with

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\mathsf{PaRB},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}})\cong\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Op}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}})\cong\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}.

It follows that
\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}.

∎

5.2.
Homotopy Automorphisms of
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}

Every endomorphism of
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is homotopic to one that fixes objects.
Let
I
denote the groupoid completion of the poset
\{0<1\}
.
For operad morphisms
f,g\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathbf{Op}(\mathbf{G})
,
a
homotopy
between
f
and
g
is a morphism

H\colon\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}^{I}

such that the compositions with the evaluation maps

d_{0},d_{1}\colon\mathcal{Q}^{I}\to\mathcal{Q}
satisfy

d_{0}\circ H=f
and
d_{1}\circ H=g
.
The object
\mathcal{Q}^{I}
serves as a categorical path object in
\mathbf{Op}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})
,
and the same definition applies verbatim in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})
.

We write
\operatorname{HoEnd}(\mathcal{P})
for the monoid of
homotopy endomorphisms
of an operad or cyclic operad
\mathcal{P}
in groupoids; its elements are equivalence classes of endomorphisms under the equivalence relation generated by such homotopies.

Proposition 5.5
.

Every endomorphism of the cyclic operad
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is homotopic to one that fixes the objects.

Proof.

The cyclic operad
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is the free cyclic operad generated by a single binary operation
\mu
.
Hence any morphism of cyclic operads

g\colon\operatorname{ob}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{ob}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})

is completely determined by the image of
\mu
, which must have the form
g(\mu)=\sigma^{*}\cdot\mu
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{2}^{+}
.
The underlying operad map
u^{*}(g)\colon\mathsf{PaRB}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
acts the same on the set of objects, except when
\sigma=z_{3}
is the cyclic permutation in
\Sigma_{2}^{+}
.

If
\sigma\neq z_{3}
, then the argument of
[
43
, Proposition 7.8]
shows that
u^{*}(g)
is homotopic, as an operad map, to an endomorphism
\bar{g}\colon\mathsf{PaRB}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
fixing objects and Lemma
5.2
says that this map lifts uniquely to an object-fixing map of cyclic operads
\tilde{g}\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
.

If
\sigma=z_{3}
, we construct an explicit homotopy
H\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{I}

from
g
to a map of cyclic operads that fixes objects. Let
h\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
denote the automorphism
defined on objects by
h(x)=(z_{3}^{*})^{-1}(x)
and on morphisms by
h(\gamma)=(z_{3}^{*})^{-1}(\gamma)
, that is, by inverse cyclic rotation.
Set
g_{0}=g
and
g_{1}=h^{-1}\circ g\circ h
.
This construction defines a morphism
H\colon\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\to(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{I}
with
d_{0}\circ H=g_{0}
,
d_{1}\circ H=g_{1}
i.e. a homotopy in
\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})
from
g
to the object-fixing map
g_{1}
.

The result now follows from the identification
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\operatorname{End}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
.

∎

Corollary 5.6
.

There are natural isomorphisms of profinite monoids

\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\operatorname{HoEnd}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}.

Passing to invertible elements yields an isomorphism of profinite groups

\operatorname{HoAut}_{\mathbf{Cyc}}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}.

In the next part of the paper we will show that this
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
action extends through homotopy automorphisms to a
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
action on a cyclic
\infty
operad of framed configurations which is homotopic, as cyclic
\infty
-operads, to the cyclic operad of framed little discs described in Example
2.6
.

5.3.
Galois actions on a cyclic
\infty
-operad of configuration spaces

The goal of this section is to show that the profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acts, through homotopy automorphisms, on the cyclic
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces
\widehat{\textnormal{N}\mathsf{S}_{0}}.
As a first step, we will move from the action of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
on the homotopy automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}
described in Corollary
5.6
to an action of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
on the monoid of path components of the derived mapping space
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}},\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}})
.

Corollary 5.7
.

There is an isomorphism of monoids

\operatorname{HoEnd}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
the cyclic operad
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
is cofibrant in the simplicial model structure on
\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})
and each
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\text{cyc}}(n)
has finitely many components. Profinite completion is the left adjoint in a simplicial Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\mathbf{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cyc}(\widehat{\mathbf{G}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|}

and, as such, we can identify

(5.11)

\operatorname{HoEnd}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},|\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}|^{\mathrm{cyc}}).

The cyclic dendroidal nerve is homotopically fully faithful, meaning that we have a weak equivalence of mapping spaces
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},|\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}|^{\mathrm{cyc}})\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\textnormal{N}|\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}|^{\mathrm{cyc}})
. If we now apply profinite completion and use Lemma
4.40
to commute profinite completion and the nerve functor, then we have the following sequence of weak equivalences

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\textnormal{N}|\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}|)\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\widehat{\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}).

Putting this all together, we have

\operatorname{HoEnd}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}},\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}),

as required.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
X\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{S}

be a cyclic
\infty
-operad such that, for every corolla
C_{n+1}\in\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
,
the simplicial set
X_{C_{n+1}}
is connected,
\pi
-finite and all of its homotopy groups are good.
Then the entrywise profinite completion

\widehat{X}\colon(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{S}}

is again a cyclic
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.

Proof.

The assumptions ensure that each
X_{C_{n+1}}
is
\pi
-finite and that the canonical maps

\widehat{X_{C_{m+1}}\times X_{C_{n+1}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{X}_{C_{m+1}}\times\widehat{X}_{C_{n+1}}

are weak equivalences of profinite spaces by
Proposition
4.37
.
Since profinite completion preserves terminal objects,
the image of every Segal map under
\widehat{(-)}

remains a weak equivalence in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.
Hence
\widehat{X}
satisfies the Segal condition and defines
a cyclic
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.
∎

The previous lemma provided sufficient conditions for a cyclic dendroidal object in profinite spaces to define a cyclic
\infty
-operad. Note that for a full classification one would require a cyclic version of the work on profinite
\infty
-operads of
[
11
]
.

We can now apply these criteria to the case of the cyclic operad
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
in groupoids. In Theorem
4.29
we showed that
\textnormal{N}\mathsf{S}_{0}
is a cyclic
\infty
-operad in spaces whose value at each corolla is weakly equivalent to the space of framed configurations in the complex plane. The following lemma shows that this structure is preserved under profinite completion, so that the cyclic
\infty
-operad modelling framed configurations remains a cyclic
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.

Lemma 5.9
.

The composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}}
B
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}

defines a cyclic
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.8
, it suffices to verify that, for each
n\geq 1
, the simplicial set
\textnormal{B}\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)
is connected,
\pi
-finite, and has good homotopy groups. Each groupoid
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)
is finite, and the automorphism group at any object in
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}(n)
is the pure ribbon braid group
\mathsf{PRB}_{n}\cong\Gamma_{0,n+1}
, which is known to be a
good group (see, for instance,
[
33
, Proposition 3.5]
).

It follows that each evaluation
(\textnormal{B}\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}})_{C_{n+1}}
is a connected
\pi
-finite space with good homotopy groups. Since the functor
B
preserves finite products and Proposition
4.37
implies that profinite completion preserves finite products up to weak equivalence, the profinite completion
\widehat{\textnormal{B}\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}}
inherits the structure of a cyclic
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.

∎

Lemma 5.10
.

For each corolla
C_{n+1}
in
\Omega^{\mathrm{cyc}}
there is a weak equivalence of profinite spaces

\widehat{\big(\textnormal{B}\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\big)}_{C_{n+1}}\simeq\textnormal{B}\big(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\big)_{C_{n+1}}

in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.

Proof.

By definition of the nerve we have
\widehat{\big(\textnormal{B}\textnormal{N}\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\big)}_{C_{n+1}}\simeq\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PaRB}}(n)
and
\textnormal{B}\big(\textnormal{N}\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\big)_{C_{n+1}}\simeq\textnormal{B}\big(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}(n)\big)
. Each groupoid
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
is finite with automorphism group
\mathsf{PRB}_{n}\cong\Gamma_{0,n+1}
, which is a good group. By
[
43
, Proposition 5.9]
, for such groupoids the canonical map

\widehat{\textnormal{B}\mathsf{PaRB}(n)}\ \longrightarrow\ \textnormal{B}\big(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}(n)}\big)

is a weak equivalence in
